```
usage: desloppify [-h] [--lang LANG] [--exclude PATTERN] [--version]
                  {scan,status,tree,show,next,resolve,ignore,exclude,fix,plan,viz,detect,move,review,zone,config,dev,langs,update-skill}
                  ...

Desloppify — codebase health tracker

positional arguments:
  {scan,status,tree,show,next,resolve,ignore,exclude,fix,plan,viz,detect,move,review,zone,config,dev,langs,update-skill}
    scan                Run all detectors, update state, show diff
    status              Score dashboard with per-tier progress
    tree                Annotated codebase tree (text)
    show                Dig into findings by file, directory, detector, or ID
    next                Show next highest-priority open finding
    resolve             Mark finding(s) as fixed/wontfix/false_positive/open
    ignore              Add pattern to ignore list, remove matching findings
    exclude             Add path pattern to exclude list
    fix                 Auto-fix mechanical issues
    plan                Living plan: generate, reorder, cluster, skip, note
    viz                 Generate interactive HTML treemap
    detect              Run a single detector directly (bypass state)
    move                Move a file or directory and update all import
                        references
    review              Prepare or import holistic subjective review
    zone                Show/set/clear zone classifications
    config              Show/set/unset project configuration
    dev                 Developer utilities
    langs               List all available language plugins with depth and
                        tools
    update-skill        Install or update the desloppify skill/agent document

options:
  -h, --help            show this help message and exit
  --lang LANG           Language to scan (bash, clojure, csharp, cxx, dart,
                        elixir, erlang, fsharp, gdscript, go, haskell, java,
                        javascript, kotlin, lua, nim, ocaml, perl, php,
                        powershell, python, r, ruby, rust, scala, swift,
                        typescript, zig). Auto-detected if omitted.
  --exclude PATTERN     Path pattern to exclude (component/prefix match;
                        repeatable)
  --version, -V         show program's version number and exit

core workflow:
  scan       Run detectors, update state, show diff
  status     Score dashboard with dimension health
  next       Show next highest-priority item to work on
  resolve    Mark finding(s) as fixed/wontfix/false_positive
  plan       Living plan: prioritize, cluster, skip, annotate

investigation:
  show       Dig into findings by file/dir/detector/ID
  tree       Annotated codebase tree (zoom with --focus)
  detect     Run a single detector directly (bypass state)

maintenance:
  fix        Auto-fix mechanical issues
  ignore     Suppress findings matching a pattern
  exclude    Exclude path pattern from scanning
  move       Move file/dir and update import references
  review     Holistic subjective review (LLM-based)

setup & admin:
  zone       Show/set zone classifications
  config     Project configuration
  viz        Interactive HTML treemap
  langs      List language plugins
  dev        Developer utilities
  update-skill  Install/update agent skill document

examples:
  desloppify scan
  desloppify scan --skip-slow --profile ci
  desloppify plan                        # full prioritized markdown
  desloppify plan queue                  # compact table of all items
  desloppify next --count 10             # top 10 queue items
  desloppify show src/components/Modal.tsx
  desloppify resolve fixed "unused::src/foo.tsx::React" \
    --note "removed import" --attest "I have actually ..."
  desloppify review --run-batches --parallel --scan-after-import
```

tool_result Bash
```
usage: desloppify plan [-h] [--state STATE] [--output FILE]
                       {show,queue,reset,move,describe,note,skip,unskip,done,reopen,focus,cluster}
                       ...

Manage the living plan — a persistent layer on top of the work queue.
Track custom ordering, clusters, skips, and per-finding annotations.
Run with no subcommand to generate a full prioritized markdown plan.

positional arguments:
  {show,queue,reset,move,describe,note,skip,unskip,done,reopen,focus,cluster}
    show                Show plan metadata summary
    queue               Compact table of upcoming queue items
    reset               Reset plan to empty
    move                Move findings in the queue
    describe            Set augmented description
    note                Set note on findings
    skip                Skip findings: temporary (default), --permanent
                        (wontfix), or --false-positive
    unskip              Bring skipped findings back to queue (reopens
                        permanent/fp in state)
    done                Mark findings as fixed (requires attestation, purges
                        from plan)
    reopen              Reopen resolved findings and move back to queue
    focus               Set or clear active cluster focus
    cluster             Manage finding clusters

options:
  -h, --help            show this help message and exit
  --state STATE         Path to state file
  --output FILE         Write to file instead of stdout

typical workflow:
  desloppify scan                       # detect findings
  desloppify plan                       # full prioritized markdown
  desloppify plan queue                 # compact table of all items
  desloppify plan cluster create ...    # group related findings
  desloppify plan focus <cluster>       # narrow scope
  desloppify next                       # work on the next item
  desloppify plan done <id> --attest .. # mark as fixed

subcommands:
  show       Show plan metadata summary
  queue      Compact table of upcoming queue items
  reset      Reset plan to empty
  move       Move findings in the queue
  describe   Set augmented description
  note       Set note on findings
  skip       Skip findings (temporary/permanent/false_positive)
  unskip     Bring skipped findings back to queue
  done       Mark findings as fixed (requires attestation)
  reopen     Reopen resolved findings
  focus      Set or clear active cluster focus
  cluster    Manage finding clusters
```

usage: desloppify next [-h] [--state STATE] [--tier {1,2,3,4}] [--count COUNT]
                       [--scope SCOPE]
                       [--status {open,fixed,wontfix,false_positive,auto_resolved,all}]
                       [--group {item,file,detector,tier}]
                       [--format {terminal,json,md}] [--explain]
                       [--no-tier-fallback] [--cluster NAME]

options:
  -h, --help            show this help message and exit
  --state STATE         Path to state file
  --tier {1,2,3,4}      Show only this tier
  --count COUNT         Number of items to show (default: 1)
  --scope SCOPE         Optional scope filter (path, detector, ID prefix, or
                        glob)
  --status {open,fixed,wontfix,false_positive,auto_resolved,all}
                        Status filter for queue items (default: open)
  --group {item,file,detector,tier}
                        Group output by item, file, detector, or tier
  --format {terminal,json,md}
                        Output format (default: terminal)
  --explain             Show ranking and tier-fallback rationale
  --no-tier-fallback    Do not auto-fallback to another tier when --tier has
                        no items
  --cluster NAME        Filter to a specific plan cluster
  --include-skipped     Include skipped items in the queue
  --output FILE         Write JSON/Markdown to file (with --format json|md)

examples:
  desloppify next                       # single highest-priority item
  desloppify next --count 10            # top 10 items
  desloppify next --tier 1              # only tier 1
  desloppify next --group file          # group by file
  desloppify next --cluster my-cluster  # items in a cluster

usage: desloppify resolve [-h] [--note NOTE] [--attest ATTEST]
                          [--confirm-batch-wontfix] [--state STATE]

                          [PATTERN ...]

positional arguments:
  {open,fixed,wontfix,false_positive}

                        glob

options:
  -h, --help            show this help message and exit
  --note NOTE           Explanation (required for wontfix)
  --attest ATTEST       Required anti-gaming attestation. Must include BOTH
                        keywords 'I have actually' and 'not gaming'. Example:
                        --attest "I have actually [DESCRIBE THE CONCRETE
                        CHANGE YOU MADE] and I am not gaming the score by
                        resolving without fixing."
  --confirm-batch-wontfix
                        Confirm large wontfix batch (>10 findings)
  --state STATE         Path to state file

examples:
  desloppify resolve fixed "unused::src/foo.tsx::React" \
    --note "removed import" --attest "I have actually ..."
  desloppify resolve wontfix deprecated \
    --note "tracked in migration" --attest "I have actually ..."
  desloppify resolve fixed "logs::src/foo.tsx::*" --note "removed debug logs" \
    --attest "I have actually ..."

> DEVELOPER

You are testing the desloppify CLI's investigation workflow. You're an agent who wants to understand what's wrong with the codebase, dig into specific areas, and decide what to focus on.

Run these commands in sequence and evaluate the experience:

1. Get the overall status: `python -m desloppify --lang python status 2>&1`
2. See the queue breakdown: `python -m desloppify --lang python plan queue --top 15 2>&1`
3. Show all findings for a specific detector: `python -m desloppify --lang python show smells --top 10 2>&1`
4. Show findings by file — pick one from the output above: `python -m desloppify --lang python show desloppify/engine/_work_queue/core.py 2>&1`
5. Show help to understand filtering: `python -m desloppify show --help 2>&1`
6. Try chronic findings: `python -m desloppify --lang python show --chronic 2>&1`
7. Try status command with JSON: `python -m desloppify --lang python status --json 2>&1 | python -m json.tool | head -40`
8. Look at the full plan: `python -m desloppify --lang python plan 2>&1 | head -80`

After running all of these, write a detailed CRITIQUE:
- Could you quickly understand the health of the codebase?
- Was it clear how to drill down from overview → specific findings?
- Is `plan queue` vs `status` vs `plan` (full markdown) clear in their different purposes?
- Was the `show` command flexible enough for investigation?
- Any missing views or filters you wished existed?
- How well do the different commands complement each other vs overlap?
- Rate the investigation workflow experience 1-10

Do NOT modify any files. This is a read-only evaluation.

tool_result Bash
```
usage: desloppify plan [-h] [--state STATE] [--output FILE]
                       {show,queue,reset,move,describe,note,skip,unskip,done,reopen,focus,cluster}
                       ...

Manage the living plan — a persistent layer on top of the work queue.
Track custom ordering, clusters, skips, and per-finding annotations.
Run with no subcommand to generate a full prioritized markdown plan.

positional arguments:
  {show,queue,reset,move,describe,note,skip,unskip,done,reopen,focus,cluster}
    show                Show plan metadata summary
    queue               Compact table of upcoming queue items
    reset               Reset plan to empty
    move                Move findings in the queue
    describe            Set augmented description
    note                Set note on findings
    skip                Skip findings: temporary (default), --permanent
                        (wontfix), or --false-positive
    unskip              Bring skipped findings back to queue (reopens
                        permanent/fp in state)
    done                Mark findings as fixed (requires attestation, purges
                        from plan)
    reopen              Reopen resolved findings and move back to queue
    focus               Set or clear active cluster focus
    cluster             Manage finding clusters

options:
  -h, --help            show this help message and exit
  --state STATE         Path to state file
  --output FILE         Write to file instead of stdout

typical workflow:
  desloppify scan                       # detect findings
  desloppify plan                       # full prioritized markdown
  desloppify plan queue                 # compact table of all items
  desloppify plan cluster create ...    # group related findings
  desloppify plan focus <cluster>       # narrow scope
  desloppify next                       # work on the next item
  desloppify plan done <id> --attest .. # mark as fixed

subcommands:
  show       Show plan metadata summary
  queue      Compact table of upcoming queue items
  reset      Reset plan to empty
  move       Move findings in the queue
  describe   Set augmented description
  note       Set note on findings
  skip       Skip findings (temporary/permanent/false_positive)
  unskip     Bring skipped findings back to queue
  done       Mark findings as fixed (requires attestation)
  reopen     Reopen resolved findings
  focus      Set or clear active cluster focus
  cluster    Manage finding clusters
```

  --attest ATTEST       Required anti-gaming attestation. Must include BOTH
                        keywords 'I have actually' and 'not gaming'. Example:
                        --attest "I have actually [DESCRIBE THE CONCRETE
                        CHANGE YOU MADE] and I am not gaming the score by
                        resolving without fixing."
  --confirm-batch-wontfix
                        Confirm large wontfix batch (>10 findings)
  --state STATE         Path to state file

examples:
  desloppify resolve fixed "unused::src/foo.tsx::React" \
    --note "removed import" --attest "I have actually ..."
  desloppify resolve wontfix deprecated \
    --note "tracked in migration" --attest "I have actually ..."
  desloppify resolve fixed "logs::src/foo.tsx::*" --note "removed debug logs" \
    --attest "I have actually ..."

Good. I now have all the help output I need. Here is my critique.

---

## Discoverability Critique: desloppify `--help` Evaluation

### Approach
I approached this as a new agent with zero prior knowledge, reading only `--help` output to answer four questions: (1) What is this tool? (2) How do I see what's wrong? (3) How do I prioritize work? (4) How do I mark things as done?

---

### Question 1: What is this tool and what's the basic workflow?

**Top-level help verdict: Very good.**

The tagline "Desloppify -- codebase health tracker" is concise and immediately communicative. The top-level help is exceptionally well-organized with four labeled sections:

- **core workflow** (scan, status, next, resolve, plan)
- **investigation** (show, tree, detect)
- **maintenance** (fix, ignore, exclude, move, review)
- **setup & admin** (zone, config, viz, langs, dev, update-skill)

This categorical grouping is the single best design decision in the help system. From this alone, I can immediately infer the basic workflow: `scan` to find issues, `plan`/`next` to prioritize, `resolve` to close them. The **examples section** at the bottom reinforces this by showing a realistic sequence of commands.

**One minor issue:** The top-level description says "codebase health tracker" but never explains *what kind* of health. Is it linting? Security? Complexity? Code smells? Dead code? The answer is apparently "all of the above," but a new user has to infer this. A single extra line like "Detects code smells, duplication, unused code, and other quality issues across 28+ languages" would help enormously.

---

### Question 2: How do I see what's wrong with my codebase?

**Answer: Run `scan`, then use `show`, `status`, or `plan` to explore.**

This was straightforward to figure out. The `scan` description ("Run all detectors, update state, show diff") tells me it both detects AND persists results. The `show` command's help makes it clear I can drill down by file, directory, detector, or ID. The `status` command gives a dashboard.

**Confusion point:** The `scan` help mentions `--profile {objective,full,ci}` but does not explain what these profiles differ in. What is "objective" vs "full"? What does "ci" skip or add? A one-line description per profile would eliminate guesswork.

**Confusion point:** The `scan` help mentions `--reset-subjective` but never explains what "subjective measures" are or when you'd want to reset them. This is insider jargon.

**Confusion point:** `--force-resolve` says "Bypass suspect-detector protection (use when a detector legitimately went to 0)." This is confusing to a newcomer. What is "suspect-detector protection"? Why would a detector "go to 0"? The parenthetical tries to help but assumes knowledge of an internal safety mechanism.

---

### Question 3: How do I prioritize work?

**Answer: `plan` for full breakdown, `plan queue` for compact table, `next` for single item.**

This was the **clearest part of the entire help system**. The `plan --help` includes a "typical workflow" section that spells out the exact sequence:

```
desloppify scan                       # detect findings
desloppify plan                       # full prioritized markdown
desloppify plan queue                 # compact table of all items
desloppify plan cluster create ...    # group related findings
desloppify plan focus <cluster>       # narrow scope
desloppify next                       # work on the next item
desloppify plan done <id> --attest .. # mark as fixed
```

This is excellent. It answers "what do I do first?" and "what do I do next?" in a linear, numbered-feeling sequence. The `next --help` is also well-designed with clear examples showing `--count`, `--tier`, `--group`, and `--cluster` as progressive filters.

**Minor confusion:** The relationship between `next` and `plan queue` is not immediately obvious. Both seem to show "what to work on next." The difference appears to be that `next` shows 1 item by default (with ranking logic) while `plan queue` shows a compact table. It would help to have a one-liner in each help distinguishing them: e.g., `next` says "Uses priority ranking to pick the single best item" and `plan queue` says "Shows the full ordered queue as a table (use `next` for the AI-ranked top pick)."

**Confusion point:** The `next` help mentions `--tier {1,2,3,4}` but never explains what tiers are. Is tier 1 the most critical or the least? How are tiers assigned? A single line like "Tiers: 1=critical, 2=important, 3=moderate, 4=cosmetic" would resolve this instantly.

---

### Question 4: How do I mark things as done?

**Answer: Two paths -- `resolve fixed <ID>` or `plan done <ID>`.**

This is where the most significant confusion arises. There are **two different commands** that mark things as fixed:

1. `desloppify resolve fixed <pattern> --attest "..."` (from the core `resolve` command)
2. `desloppify plan done <id> --attest "..."` (from the plan subcommand)

The help does not explain the relationship between these two. Are they equivalent? Does `plan done` call `resolve` under the hood? Should I prefer one over the other? The "typical workflow" in `plan --help` shows `plan done`, but the top-level examples show `resolve fixed`. A new user would be confused about which to use.

**The `--attest` flag is the most confusing element in the entire help system.** The `resolve --help` says:

> Required anti-gaming attestation. Must include BOTH keywords 'I have actually' and 'not gaming'. Example: --attest "I have actually [DESCRIBE THE CONCRETE CHANGE YOU MADE] and I am not gaming the score by resolving without fixing."

This is a major discoverability barrier:

- The concept of "anti-gaming" is introduced with no context. Gaming what? For a new user, this is bewildering. It implies there is a scoring system that can be gamed, but this is never explained in any `--help`.
- The requirement to include specific magic keywords ("I have actually" AND "not gaming") is unusual and surprising. It reads like a CAPTCHA for CLI tools.
- The example string is 20+ words long, which makes the command line unwieldy. A new agent would likely get this wrong on the first try (missing one keyword, wrong phrasing, etc.).

I understand the design intent -- forcing the agent to attest it actually made a change rather than just gaming a score. But from a discoverability standpoint, this needs more context in the help about *why* this exists.

---

### Additional Observations

**What's missing from help:**

1. **No description of "findings."** The entire tool revolves around "findings" but no help text ever defines what a finding is. Is it a lint warning? A code smell? A bug? An agent unfamiliar with the domain has to guess.

2. **No description of the scoring system.** The `status` command shows a "score dashboard with per-tier progress" but the help never explains what the score measures or how it's calculated. The `--attest` flag references "gaming the score" but the score itself is undocumented in help.

3. **No `--lang` guidance.** The top-level help says `--lang` is "auto-detected if omitted" which is good. But if auto-detection fails, there is no help on how to set it or what it controls.

4. **`scan --help` has no description line.** Most subcommands have a one-line description at the top of their help. `scan` jumps straight into options with no prose. Compare to `plan --help` which has a helpful 3-line description paragraph -- that pattern should be applied to `scan` as well.

5. **`status --help` is extremely sparse.** Just `--state` and `--json`. No description, no examples. For a "core workflow" command, this is underwhelming. What does the dashboard look like? What dimensions does it show?

6. **The `show --notes FILE` flag is cryptic.** "Path to investigation notes file to attach to a finding" -- attach how? Is this a file I create? What format? When would I use this?

---

### What Was Clearest

1. **The categorical grouping** in top-level help (core workflow / investigation / maintenance / setup & admin) is outstanding. It immediately conveys the tool's mental model.
2. **The `plan --help` typical workflow section** is the gold standard for CLI help. It answers "what do I do?" in a linear sequence with comments.
3. **The examples** throughout are realistic and progressive (simple case first, then more complex flags).
4. **The `next` command** is perfectly named and its help is well-structured with clear filter options.

### What Was Least Clear

1. **The `resolve` vs `plan done` duality** -- which one should I use?
2. **The `--attest` mechanism** -- what is anti-gaming, why does it exist, and why do I need magic keywords?
3. **Tiers** -- referenced in multiple commands but never defined.
4. **Profiles** (`--profile objective/full/ci`) -- completely unexplained.
5. **"Subjective measures"** -- insider terminology with no definition.
6. **"Findings"** -- the core data type is never defined.

---

### Overall Discoverability Rating: 7 / 10

**Rationale:** The top-level organization is genuinely excellent and the plan workflow documentation is best-in-class for a CLI tool. A new agent can figure out the basic scan-plan-next-resolve cycle from help alone, which is the most important thing. The tool loses points for: (a) two overlapping paths to mark things done with no guidance on which to prefer, (b) the `--attest` mechanism being confusing without context, (c) core concepts (findings, tiers, scoring, profiles) being referenced but never defined in help text, and (d) some commands (`status`, `scan`) having much sparser help than others (`plan`, `next`). With a glossary or a few extra descriptive lines in key places, this could easily be an 8 or 9.

> AGENT

All 8 commands have been run. Here is my detailed critique.

---

## Critique: "Work on the Next Thing" Workflow

### 1. Was it clear what the "next" item is and why it was chosen?

**Partially.** The `next` command clearly presents a single item -- "Remove 3 unused findings" -- with files, sample finding IDs, and a suggested action (`desloppify fix unused-imports --dry-run`). That part is good. However, **why** this item was chosen over the 265 others is not explained at all. There is no ranking rationale. The Tier Navigator at the top shows `T1:7 T2:107 T3:439 T4:260`, which suggests there are 7 tier-1 items, but when I ran `--tier 1` it gave me the same item (which the `--group detector` view revealed is T3). This is confusing -- if there are 7 T1 items, why isn't the default `next` showing one of them? Either the tier counts are misleading, or the `--tier 1` flag doesn't do what I expected it to do (filter to only tier 1 items). Looking at the `--tier 1` output, it shows the exact same item as the unfiltered `next`, so the flag appears to be broken or have different semantics than "show me only tier 1 items."

**Score impact hint is missing.** It would be helpful if the next item said something like "Fixing this will move strict score from 90.4 to ~90.6" to motivate the work.

### 2. Is the relationship between `next` and `plan queue` obvious?

**Yes, reasonably.** They clearly show the same items in the same order. `plan queue` is a compact table view (one line per cluster), while `next` expands each item with files, samples, and action commands. They complement each other well. The queue view is great for "what's the lay of the land" and next is great for "tell me exactly what to do."

One minor issue: the queue view doesn't show tier values for any of the 5 items (the Tier column is blank), which undermines its usefulness as a triage surface.

### 3. Was it clear how to resolve/skip/done an item after seeing it?

**Mostly, but with significant friction.** Each `next` card includes three action lines:

```
Resolve all:   desloppify resolve fixed "auto/unused" --note "<what>" --attest "..."
Drill in:      desloppify next --cluster auto/unused --count 10
Skip cluster:  desloppify plan skip auto/unused
```

The `resolve` command is pre-filled with the cluster pattern, which is helpful. But the `--attest "..."` placeholder is vague. Looking at `resolve --help`, the attestation must include BOTH keywords "I have actually" and "not gaming." This is a very specific requirement that the `next` output hints at but doesn't fully spell out. An agent or user who copies the template and fills in `--attest "done"` would get a rejection. The template should include the required keywords, e.g.:

```
--attest "I have actually removed the unused imports and am not gaming the score"
```

### 4. Were there too many ways to "complete" something?

**Yes, this is the biggest confusion point.** There are at least three ways to mark something done:

1. `desloppify resolve fixed <pattern> --note "..." --attest "..."` -- from the resolve subsystem
2. `desloppify plan done <pattern> --attest "..."` -- from the plan subsystem
3. `desloppify plan skip <pattern>` -- for temporary deferral (plus `--permanent` for wontfix)

The `next` output only suggests `resolve fixed` and `plan skip`. It never mentions `plan done`. So what is the difference between `resolve fixed` and `plan done`? The help texts don't clarify this at all. `plan done` requires `--attest` but not `--note`. `resolve fixed` requires both `--note` and `--attest`. Are they equivalent? Does one supersede the other? Does `plan done` also update the state file, or just the plan file? This is genuinely confusing.

Similarly, `plan skip` (temporary) vs `resolve wontfix` vs `plan skip --permanent` seem to overlap. The user has to understand the internal difference between the "plan" system and the "state/resolve" system, which is a leaky abstraction.

### 5. Any confusion about the attestation requirement?

**Yes, considerable.** The attestation is an anti-gaming mechanism, which is a good idea in principle. But:

- The `next` output shows `--attest "..."` without explaining the keyword requirement.
- The `resolve --help` spells out the requirement ("Must include BOTH keywords 'I have actually' and 'not gaming'"), but this is only in the help text, not inline.
- `plan done --help` says `--attest ATTEST` is "required" but gives no format guidance at all.
- `plan skip --help` says attestation is required for `--permanent` and `--false-positive` but again no format guidance.
- It's unclear whether `plan done` has the same keyword validation as `resolve`.

For an AI agent, this is manageable (read the help, follow the template). For a human, the ceremony of typing "I have actually removed unused imports and am not gaming the score" every time feels heavy for small fixes. There is no shorthand, no `--yes-really` flag, no way to batch-attest.

### 6. What's the smoothest path from "see item -> work on it -> mark done"?

The smoothest path right now is:

1. `desloppify next` -- see the item
2. Actually do the work (e.g., run `desloppify fix unused-imports`)
3. `desloppify resolve fixed "auto/unused" --note "removed unused imports" --attest "I have actually removed the unused imports and am not gaming the score"`
4. `desloppify scan` (re-scan to confirm)

This is 4 steps, which is reasonable. But step 3 is verbose and error-prone. The command template in `next` output should be copy-pasteable with only minimal editing. Right now it requires filling in two placeholders (`<what>` and `"..."`), and the attestation placeholder doesn't include the required keywords.

**Ideal improvement:** The `next` output should emit a ready-to-edit command like:

```
desloppify resolve fixed "auto/unused" \
  --note "removed unused imports from 2 files" \
  --attest "I have actually removed the unused imports and am not gaming the score"
```

Or better yet, a `desloppify done` shorthand that wraps `resolve fixed` with a pre-filled attestation template based on the finding type.

### 7. Other observations

- **The `--group detector` view is excellent.** It gives a great bird's-eye view organized by detector family. The compact one-liner format per cluster with tier/priority tags is very scannable. This might actually be the best entry point for an agent deciding what to work on.

- **The "North star" footer is motivating.** Showing `90.4/100 -> target 98.0 (+7.6 needed)` gives a clear sense of progress and remaining work.

- **The "Subjective" footer is intriguing but actionable.** "15 dimensions below target, 12 stale, 10 review findings open, 230 files need review" hints at a parallel review system, but it's not clear how that relates to the `next` queue.

- **Warning noise.** Every command starts with "Tool code changed since last scan" and "skill document is outdated" warnings. These are useful once but become noise on repeated invocations. They should probably be shown once per session or suppressed with a flag.

- **The `--tier 1` flag behavior is confusing.** As noted, it showed the same T3 item even though the navigator says there are 7 T1 items. Either the flag is not working as a filter, or the tier counts in the navigator mean something different than I expect.

### Overall Rating: 6.5/10

**What works well:**
- The `next` card layout is clear and information-rich
- File list + sample finding IDs make the scope concrete
- Action commands are pre-filled with the right patterns
- `plan queue` and `next --group detector` provide good complementary views
- The tier navigator and north star score give context

**What needs improvement:**
- The `resolve fixed` vs `plan done` distinction is confusing and undocumented
- The attestation template in `next` output is incomplete (missing required keywords)
- `--tier 1` filtering appears broken or misleading
- The queue table is missing tier values
- No ranking rationale ("why this item first?")
- No score-impact preview ("fixing this gains +X points")
- Too much warning noise on every invocation
- The ceremony for marking a 3-line unused import fix as "done" is disproportionately heavy

The bones of the workflow are solid. The main gaps are in reducing friction at the "mark it done" step and eliminating the confusion between the plan and resolve subsystems. A unified `desloppify done <pattern>` command that handles both systems would dramatically simplify the experience.

Here is my detailed critique of the queue management workflow:

## Critique: Desloppify Queue Management Workflow

### 1. Was the queue output clear enough to understand what to work on?

**Partially, but with significant gaps.** The `plan queue` output shows a table with columns `#`, `Tier`, `Detector`, `Summary`, and `Cluster`, but there are two critical problems:

- **The `#` and `Tier` columns are blank for all clustered items.** The first ~30 rows of the queue (the clusters) have no queue position number and no tier indicator. You can see from the header line `Queue: 266 items (T1:7 T2:107 T3:439 T4:260)` that tiers exist, but the individual clustered rows do not show them. This makes it impossible to know whether the first cluster (`auto/unused` with 3 items) is T1, T2, or a mix. Only the unclustered individual items at the bottom (starting at `#60`) show tier and position.

- **Cluster summaries are extremely generic.** Lines like `[36 items] Fix 36 phantom read issues` or `[16 items] Fix 16 file issues` do not give enough information to decide priority. Which files? How severe? The `next` command does a much better job by showing sample files and specific finding IDs.

- **No effort/impact signal.** There is no indication of estimated effort, impact on score, or which dimension each cluster maps to. The `plan` (full markdown) output has tier and dimension info in its health table, but the queue table lacks it.

### 2. Could you figure out how to reorder/prioritize from the help?

**Yes, the `plan move` help is reasonably clear.** The syntax `PATTERN {top,bottom,before,after,up,down} [target]` is concise and the pattern can be a finding ID, prefix, detector name, file path, or glob. This is flexible. However:

- **No examples in the help text.** The help tells you the grammar but not how to actually use it. Something like `desloppify plan move smells-monster_function top` or `desloppify plan move 60 before 85` would make this immediately actionable.

- **Unclear how to move clusters.** The `plan move` help talks about "finding IDs" but the queue shows clusters as `auto/unused`. It is not obvious whether you should use `plan move auto/unused top` or `plan cluster move auto/unused top`. The existence of both `plan move` and `plan cluster move` is confusing without examples showing the difference.

### 3. Was the relationship between `plan queue`, `plan show`, and `next` clear?

**No, this is the biggest confusion point in the workflow.** The three commands have overlapping responsibilities and it is not obvious when to use which:

- **`plan queue`** shows a compact table of upcoming work items, with clusters collapsed into single rows.
- **`plan show`** shows plan metadata: ordered item count, cluster list, override and superseded counts. This is more of a "plan health check" than an actionable view.
- **`plan` (no subcommand)** generates a full markdown plan with the health table, tier breakdown, and all items. This is the most comprehensive but also the most verbose.
- **`next`** shows the single top-priority item with actionable commands (fix, resolve, skip, drill-in).

The confusion: `plan queue` and `next` both show "what to work on next" but at different granularity. `plan show` and `plan` (no subcommand) both show plan metadata but in different formats. A user would not know which to reach for without trial and error.

Specific overlaps/gaps:
- `plan show` lists 36 clusters with item counts. `plan queue` also lists clusters with item counts. The information is nearly identical but formatted differently.
- `plan queue` shows `Queue: 266 items` but `plan show` says `Ordered items: 12` and lists clusters with item counts that add up to far more than 266. The discrepancy (266 queue items vs. 12 ordered items vs. 36 clusters) is confusing without understanding the internal distinction between "ordered", "clustered", and "in the queue."
- `next` shows the first item from the queue but does not reference its position number from `plan queue`. If the queue says the first item is `auto/unused`, `next` correctly shows it, but there is no cross-reference (e.g., "Queue position #1").

### 4. Confusing overlaps or gaps between commands

- **`plan move` vs. `plan cluster move`**: Both can reorder things. It is unclear when you would use one vs. the other. `plan move` accepts "detector name" as a pattern, so `plan move unused top` might work for individual findings, while `plan cluster move auto/unused top` moves the cluster as a block. But this distinction is not documented.
- **`plan queue --cluster NAME` vs `plan cluster show NAME`**: Both seem to let you inspect a cluster's contents. The overlap is not explained.
- **`plan done` vs. `desloppify resolve`**: The help mentions `plan done <id> --attest`, but `next` shows `desloppify resolve fixed "auto/unused" --note "<what>" --attest "..."`. The relationship between these two resolution paths is unclear.
- **Gap: No `plan queue --tier N` filter.** You can filter by cluster (`--cluster NAME`) but not by tier. Given the tier breakdown in the header, being able to say `plan queue --tier 1` would be natural.
- **Gap: No `plan queue --detector NAME` filter.** You can see detector names in the output but cannot filter by them.

### 5. Were cluster numbers/items/tiers displayed clearly enough?

**No.** Key issues:

- Cluster rows in `plan queue` show no tier. A cluster with 36 items could span T1-T4 and you would not know.
- Cluster rows show no position number (`#` column is blank). This means you cannot reference them by position in `plan move`.
- The cluster name format (`auto/smells-high_cyclomatic_complexity`) is long and wraps awkwardly in the table. The Summary column is truncated with ellipsis (`...`).
- `plan show` lists clusters in what appears to be an arbitrary order (not matching the queue order). This is confusing when trying to cross-reference.
- Cluster item counts differ between `plan queue` and `plan show` (e.g., `test_coverage` shows 30 in queue but 31 in show). This might be due to skip/status differences but is not explained.

### 6. What would make this workflow smoother?

1. **Show tier for clustered rows.** Even if it is a range like `T1-T3`, it would help enormously.
2. **Show position numbers for all rows.** Clusters should have a position so they can be referenced in `plan move`.
3. **Add examples to all help text.** Every subcommand should show 2-3 real usage examples. The `plan --help` "typical workflow" section is a good start but the individual subcommands lack examples.
4. **Clarify `plan move` vs. `plan cluster move`.** Either merge them or clearly explain when to use each. Consider making `plan move auto/unused top` just work for clusters too.
5. **Add `--tier` and `--detector` filters to `plan queue`.** Natural filtering dimensions.
6. **Add a brief explainer to `plan show` output** explaining the difference between "ordered items" and "queue items" and "clusters."
7. **Show impact/score delta.** For each item or cluster, show the estimated score improvement if resolved. The health table in the full `plan` output has this info implicitly but it is not surfaced in the queue.
8. **Unify the resolution story.** Either `plan done` or `resolve` should be the canonical way, with the other being an alias. Having both without clear documentation of their relationship is confusing.
9. **`plan queue` should show the cluster's most important finding** (not just the count), so users can decide priority without drilling into each cluster.
10. **Cross-reference between surfaces.** `next` should say "Queue position #1" and `plan queue` should mark the current `next` item.

### 7. Rating: Reordering Workflow Experience

**5/10**

The building blocks are all there -- `plan move`, `plan cluster move`, `plan focus`, `plan skip` -- and the grammar is reasonably well-designed. But the experience falls short because:

- You cannot see enough information in `plan queue` to make informed reordering decisions (no tiers on clusters, truncated summaries, no impact estimates).
- The lack of examples in help text means you have to guess at syntax and run commands experimentally.
- The three overlapping views (`plan queue`, `plan show`, `plan` with no subcommand) create cognitive overhead without a clear "use this one for X" guide.
- Cluster rows lacking position numbers makes it unclear how to reference them in `plan move`.
- The disconnect between `plan done` and `resolve` creates uncertainty about the correct resolution path.

The foundation is solid. The subcommand structure makes sense, the pattern matching in `plan move` is flexible, and the cluster abstraction is valuable for managing large queues. With better information density in the queue table, examples in help, and clearer separation of concerns between the three plan views, this could easily be an 8/10.

All 8 commands have been executed. Here is my detailed critique of the investigation workflow.

---

## Investigation Workflow Critique

### 1. Could you quickly understand the health of the codebase?

**Yes, quite effectively.** The `status` command delivers a dense but well-organized snapshot. Within about 10 seconds of reading the output I could tell:

- Overall score: 90.4/100 (good but plateaued)
- 798 open in-scope findings across 665 files and 133K LOC
- The weakest mechanical dimensions are File health (90.0%) and Test health (92.1%)
- The weakest subjective dimensions are Abstraction fit (48.0%), Contracts (65.0%), and Error consistency (65.3%) -- these are clearly the places to focus
- The "Score recipe" section at the bottom that shows weighted drags is excellent -- it tells you exactly where your effort will have the most score impact
- The "Structural Debt by Area" table quickly identifies which directories carry the most debt

**One issue:** The status output is very long (roughly 70+ lines of terminal output). For a quick "how are we doing?" check, it might benefit from a `--brief` mode that shows just the scores, the dimension table, and the focus recommendation -- without the structural debt breakdown, workflow instructions, or agent plan footer.

### 2. Was it clear how to drill down from overview to specific findings?

**Mostly yes, with some friction.** The natural flow is:

1. `status` -- see overall health and which dimensions are weak
2. `plan queue` -- see the prioritized work items grouped by detector/smell type
3. `show <detector>` -- drill into a specific detector's findings
4. `show <file>` -- drill into a specific file

This progression works, but there is a **gap between dimensions and detectors**. The `status` command shows dimensions like "Code quality" at 96.7%, and the dimension table shows tiers and actions. But it does not tell me which detectors contribute to that dimension. I had to already know that "smells" was a detector name to use `show smells`. A user who sees "File health at 90.0%" in status would not intuitively know to type `show structural` to drill in.

**Suggestion:** Either add a `show --dimension "Code quality"` filter, or have the dimension table footnote the contributing detectors (e.g., "Code quality: smells, unused, props, dict_keys").

### 3. Is `plan queue` vs `status` vs `plan` (full markdown) clear in their different purposes?

**This is the weakest part of the workflow.** The three commands overlap significantly and their distinct purposes are not obvious:

- **`status`**: Shows scores, dimension table, structural debt by area, agent coaching. This is the "dashboard."
- **`plan`** (full markdown): Shows scores, the same dimension table (as markdown), tier breakdowns, the user-ordered queue, and remaining items. This is a "planning document."
- **`plan queue`**: Shows the auto-clustered work queue in a compact table format.

**Problems observed:**

1. **`plan queue` is missing key data.** The Tier column is empty for all rows. The output shows cluster names but no tier information, making it impossible to know the priority of each cluster. This seems like a bug or a rendering issue -- the header has "Tier" but every row is blank.

2. **`plan` and `status` duplicate the dimension table.** The full `plan` output repeats the dimension health table from `status` almost identically, just in markdown format instead of terminal-formatted. A user running both commands sees the same data twice.

3. **The "User-Ordered Queue" in `plan` is entirely subjective review items.** All 12 items are stale subjective dimension re-reviews. The 801 "Remaining (mechanical order)" items are where the actual mechanical findings live, but they are below the fold and require scrolling through the entire ordered queue first.

4. **Naming confusion.** `plan` (no subcommand) produces a markdown document. `plan queue` produces a table. `plan show` presumably exists too. The mental model of "plan is a noun (the plan document) vs plan is a verb (plan something)" is unclear.

**Suggestion:** Rename `plan` (no subcommand) to `plan report` or `plan full`, and make `plan` with no subcommand show a brief summary that explains the available subcommands.

### 4. Was the `show` command flexible enough for investigation?

**Yes, this is the strongest command.** It handles multiple query types smoothly:

- `show smells` -- filter by detector name
- `show desloppify/engine/_work_queue/core.py` -- filter by file path
- `show --chronic` -- filter by reopener status
- `show --status all` -- show all statuses

The output format is clean: findings grouped by file, with tier, confidence, line numbers, smell type, and the finding ID. The finding IDs (like `smells::desloppify/engine/_work_queue/core.py::silent_except`) are human-readable and machine-parseable.

**Issues and missing features:**

1. **Noise budget hiding is confusing.** When I ran `show smells --top 10`, it showed "Noise budget: 10/detector (158 hidden: smells: +158)" and only returned 10 results. But the `--top` flag says "Max files to show (default: 20)" -- so is the noise budget per-detector or is `--top` the limiting factor? These two limiting mechanisms interact in a way that is not transparent. I wanted to see all 168 smell findings but the noise budget silently capped me. How do I override the noise budget?

2. **No severity/confidence filter.** I can filter by detector, file, status, and chronic -- but not by confidence level (high/medium/low) or by smell sub-type (e.g., show me only `monster_function` findings, not all smells). Given that `show smells` returned a mix of monster_function, silent_except, and swallowed_error, it would be useful to filter further.

3. **No `--dimension` filter.** As noted above, I cannot say "show me everything that contributes to the File health dimension being 90%."

4. **The `--code` flag is promising** but I did not test it. Inline code snippets for findings would be very useful for investigation without leaving the CLI.

5. **No glob/directory filter beyond file paths.** The help mentions "glob" as a pattern type, but it is not clear what glob syntax is supported (e.g., `show desloppify/engine/**`).

### 5. Any missing views or filters you wished existed?

1. **Filter by smell sub-type.** `show smells --smell monster_function` or `show smells:monster_function`.
2. **Filter by dimension.** `show --dimension "File health"` to see all findings that drag down a specific dimension.
3. **Filter by tier.** `show --tier 1` or `show --tier 1,2` to see only the highest-priority items.
4. **Diff/delta view.** "What changed since last scan?" or "What new findings appeared?" -- this would be invaluable for understanding regression.
5. **A "worst files" view.** Which files have the most findings? The structural debt by area table in `status` shows directories, but not individual files. Something like `show --worst-files 10` that ranks files by total finding count/weight.
6. **Detector-to-dimension mapping.** A reference command like `detectors` or `status --detectors` that lists all detectors, how many findings each has, and which dimension each contributes to.

### 6. How well do the different commands complement each other vs overlap?

**Complementarity: Medium. Overlap: High.**

The good complementary relationships:
- `status` (overview) -> `show <detector>` (drill down) -> `show <file>` (deep dive) -- this flow works well
- `next` (what should I do right now?) is distinct from `plan` (what is the full picture?)
- `show --chronic` is a useful specialized view that does not duplicate other commands

The problematic overlaps:
- `status` and `plan` both show the dimension health table with nearly identical information
- `plan queue` and `plan` (full) both show the work queue but in different formats, and `plan queue` is arguably less useful because it lacks tier information
- The "AGENT PLAN" footer appears on every single command output (`status`, `show`, `plan queue`, even `show --chronic`). This repetition is noisy -- after the first time, it adds no value and adds 4-5 lines of clutter to every command

### 7. Overall Rating: 7/10

**Strengths:**
- The `status` command is genuinely excellent as a dashboard -- the dimension table, score recipe with weighted drags, and structural debt breakdown give a comprehensive overview
- The `show` command is flexible and well-designed for drilling into specific areas
- The `--chronic` filter is a clever feature that surfaces the most stubborn problems
- Finding IDs are well-structured and human-readable
- The JSON output mode (`--json`) produces clean, well-structured data
- The coaching/agent plan section provides actionable next steps

**Weaknesses:**
- Too much output repetition across commands (dimension table in both `status` and `plan`, agent plan footer on every command)
- `plan queue` has empty tier columns, making it much less useful than it should be
- The dimension-to-detector mapping is opaque -- you need prior knowledge to know which detector to investigate for a given dimension
- The noise budget silently hides findings without a clear way to override it
- No tier, severity, or sub-type filters on `show`
- No "what changed since last scan" delta view
- The three plan-related views (`plan`, `plan queue`, `plan show`) have unclear differentiation

The core investigation workflow of "status -> show <detector> -> show <file>" is solid and productive. The main improvements would be (a) reducing repetitive output, (b) fixing the empty tier column in `plan queue`, (c) adding a dimension-to-detector bridge, and (d) adding tier/sub-type filters to `show`.

   193→    for item in items:
   194→        cname = fid_to_cluster.get(item.get("id", ""))
   195→        if cname:
   196→            cluster_members.setdefault(cname, []).append(item)
   197→        else:
   198→            non_cluster_items.append(item)
   199→
   200→    # Build cluster meta-items
   201→    result: list[dict] = list(non_cluster_items)
   202→    for cname, members in cluster_members.items():
   203→        # Don't collapse singletons — show them as individual findings
   204→        if len(members) < 2:
   205→            result.extend(members)
   206→            continue
   207→        cluster_data = clusters.get(cname, {})
   208→        detector = members[0].get("detector", "") if members else ""
   209→        action = cluster_data.get("action") or ""
   210→        # Derive action_type from the actual cluster action, not just the detector
   211→        if "desloppify fix" in action:
   212→            action_type = "auto_fix"
   213→        elif "desloppify move" in action:
   214→            action_type = "reorganize"
   215→        else:
   216→            action_type = _action_type_for_detector(detector)
   217→            # If detector says auto_fix but cluster has no fix command, it's really refactor
   218→            if action_type == "auto_fix" and "desloppify fix" not in action:
   219→                action_type = "refactor"
   220→
   221→        # Use stored description but patch the count if it differs from visible members
   222→        stored_desc = cluster_data.get("description") or ""
   223→        total_in_cluster = len(cluster_data.get("finding_ids", []))
   224→        if stored_desc and total_in_cluster != len(members):
   225→            # Replace the stored count with the visible count
   226→            summary = stored_desc.replace(str(total_in_cluster), str(len(members)))
   227→        else:
   228→            summary = stored_desc or f"{len(members)} findings"
   229→
   230→        primary_command = cluster_data.get("action")
   231→        if not primary_command:
   232→            primary_command = f"desloppify next --cluster {cname} --count 10"
   233→
   234→        meta_item: dict = {
   235→            "id": cname,
   236→            "kind": "cluster",
   237→            "action_type": action_type,
   238→            "summary": summary,
   239→            "members": members,
   240→            "member_count": len(members),
   241→            "primary_command": primary_command,
   242→            "cluster_name": cname,
   243→            "cluster_auto": True,
   244→            "confidence": "high",
   245→            "detector": detector,
   246→            "file": "",
   247→        }
   248→        result.append(meta_item)
   249→
   250→    result.sort(key=item_sort_key)
   251→    return result
   252→
   253→
   254→def build_work_queue(
   255→    state: StateModel,
   256→    *,
   257→    options: QueueBuildOptions | None = None,
   258→) -> WorkQueueResult:
   259→    """Build ranked queue items + tier metadata."""
   260→    resolved_options = options or QueueBuildOptions()
   261→
   262→    status = resolved_options.status
   263→    if status not in ALL_STATUSES:
   264→        raise ValueError(f"Unsupported status filter: {status}")
   265→    try:
   266→        subjective_threshold_value = float(resolved_options.subjective_threshold)
   267→    except (TypeError, ValueError):
   268→        subjective_threshold_value = 100.0
   269→    subjective_threshold_value = max(0.0, min(100.0, subjective_threshold_value))
   270→
   271→    finding_items = build_finding_items(
   272→        state,
   273→        scan_path=resolved_options.scan_path,
   274→        status_filter=status,
   275→        scope=resolved_options.scope,
   276→        chronic=resolved_options.chronic,
   277→    )
   278→
   279→    all_items = list(finding_items)
   280→    if (
   281→        resolved_options.include_subjective
   282→        and status in {"open", "all"}
   283→        and not resolved_options.chronic
   284→    ):
   285→        subjective_items = build_subjective_items(
   286→            state,
   287→            state.get("findings", {}),
   288→            threshold=subjective_threshold_value,
   289→        )
   290→        for item in subjective_items:
   291→            if scope_matches(item, resolved_options.scope):
   292→                all_items.append(item)
   293→
   294→    all_items.sort(key=item_sort_key)
   295→
   296→    # Apply living plan ordering if provided
   297→    if resolved_options.plan:
   298→        all_items = _apply_plan_order(
   299→            all_items,
   300→            resolved_options.plan,
   301→            include_skipped=resolved_options.include_skipped,
   302→            cluster=resolved_options.cluster,
   303→        )
   304→
   305→    # Collapse auto-clusters into meta-items (unless drilling into a cluster)
   306→    should_collapse = (
   307→        resolved_options.collapse_clusters
   308→        and resolved_options.plan
   309→        and not resolved_options.cluster
   310→        and not resolved_options.plan.get("active_cluster")
   311→    )
   312→    if should_collapse:
   313→        all_items = _collapse_clusters(all_items, resolved_options.plan)
   314→
   315→    counts = tier_counts(all_items)
   316→
   317→    requested_tier = (
   318→        int(resolved_options.tier) if resolved_options.tier is not None else None
   319→    )
   320→    selected_tier = requested_tier
   321→    fallback_reason = None
   322→    filtered = all_items
   323→
   324→    if requested_tier is not None:
   325→        filtered = [
   326→            item
   327→            for item in all_items
   328→            if _item_matches_tier(item, requested_tier)
   329→        ]
   330→        if not filtered and not resolved_options.no_tier_fallback:
   331→            chosen = choose_fallback_tier(requested_tier, counts)
   332→            if chosen is not None:
   333→                selected_tier = chosen
   334→                filtered = [
   335→                    item
   336→                    for item in all_items
   337→                    if _item_matches_tier(item, chosen)
   338→                ]
   339→                fallback_reason = (
   340→                    f"Requested T{requested_tier} has 0 open -> showing T{chosen} "
   341→                    "(nearest non-empty)."
   342→                )
   343→        elif not filtered:
   344→            fallback_reason = f"Requested T{requested_tier} has 0 open."
   345→
   346→    total = len(filtered)
   347→    if resolved_options.count is not None and resolved_options.count > 0:
   348→        filtered = filtered[: resolved_options.count]
   349→
   350→    if resolved_options.explain:
   351→        for item in filtered:
   352→            item["explain"] = item_explain(item)
   353→
   354→    available_tiers = [tier for tier, value in counts.items() if value > 0]
   355→    return {
   356→        "items": filtered,
   357→        "total": total,
   358→        "tier_counts": counts,
   359→        "requested_tier": requested_tier,
   360→        "selected_tier": selected_tier,
   361→        "fallback_reason": fallback_reason,
   362→        "available_tiers": available_tiers,
   363→        "grouped": group_queue_items(filtered, "item"),
   364→    }
   365→
   366→
   367→__all__ = [
   368→    "ATTEST_EXAMPLE",
   369→    "QueueBuildOptions",
   370→    "WorkQueueResult",
   371→    "build_work_queue",
   372→    "group_queue_items",
   373→]
   374→

1→"""Ranking and grouping helpers for work queue selection."""
     2→
     3→from __future__ import annotations
     4→
     5→from desloppify.engine._work_queue.helpers import (
     6→    detail_dict,
     7→    is_review_finding,
     8→    is_subjective_finding,
     9→    primary_command_for_finding,
    10→    review_finding_weight,
    11→    scope_matches,
    12→    slugify,
    13→    status_matches,
    14→    subjective_strict_scores,
    15→    supported_fixers_for_item,
    16→)
    17→from desloppify.engine.planning.common import CONFIDENCE_ORDER
    18→from desloppify.state import path_scoped_findings
    19→
    20→
    21→def subjective_score_value(item: dict) -> float:
    22→    if item.get("kind") == "subjective_dimension":
    23→        detail = detail_dict(item)
    24→        return float(detail.get("strict_score", item.get("subjective_score", 100.0)))
    25→    return float(item.get("subjective_score", 100.0))
    26→
    27→
    28→def build_finding_items(
    29→    state: dict,
    30→    *,
    31→    scan_path: str | None,
    32→    status_filter: str,
    33→    scope: str | None,
    34→    chronic: bool,
    35→) -> list[dict]:
    36→    scoped = path_scoped_findings(state.get("findings", {}), scan_path)
    37→    subjective_scores = subjective_strict_scores(state)
    38→    out: list[dict] = []
    39→
    40→    for finding_id, finding in scoped.items():
    41→        if finding.get("suppressed"):
    42→            continue
    43→        if not status_matches(finding.get("status", "open"), status_filter):
    44→            continue
    45→        if chronic and not (
    46→            finding.get("status") == "open" and finding.get("reopen_count", 0) >= 2
    47→        ):
    48→            continue
    49→
    50→        item = dict(finding)
    51→        item["id"] = finding_id
    52→        item["kind"] = "finding"
    53→        item["is_review"] = is_review_finding(item)
    54→        item["is_subjective"] = is_subjective_finding(item)
    55→        item["effective_tier"] = (
    56→            4 if item["is_subjective"] else int(finding.get("tier", 3))
    57→        )
    58→        item["review_weight"] = (
    59→            review_finding_weight(item) if item["is_review"] else None
    60→        )
    61→        subjective_score = None
    62→        if item["is_subjective"]:
    63→            detail = detail_dict(finding)
    64→            dim_name = detail.get("dimension_name", "")
    65→            dim_key = detail.get("dimension", "") or slugify(dim_name)
    66→            subjective_score = subjective_scores.get(
    67→                dim_key, subjective_scores.get(dim_name.lower(), 100.0)
    68→            )
    69→        item["subjective_score"] = subjective_score
    70→        supported_fixers = supported_fixers_for_item(state, item)
    71→        item["primary_command"] = primary_command_for_finding(
    72→            item,
    73→            supported_fixers=supported_fixers,
    74→        )
    75→
    76→        if not scope_matches(item, scope):
    77→            continue
    78→        out.append(item)
    79→
    80→    return out
    81→
    82→
    83→_CLUSTER_ACTION_PRIORITY = {"auto_fix": 0, "reorganize": 1, "refactor": 2, "manual_fix": 3}
    84→
    85→
    86→def item_sort_key(item: dict) -> tuple:
    87→    if item.get("kind") == "cluster":
    88→        # Clusters sort before individual findings, ordered by action type
    89→        # (auto_fix first, then reorganize, refactor, manual_fix),
    90→        # then bigger clusters first within the same action type.
    91→        action_pri = _CLUSTER_ACTION_PRIORITY.get(
    92→            item.get("action_type", "manual_fix"), 3
    93→        )
    94→        return (
    95→            0,  # All clusters before all individual findings
    96→            action_pri,
    97→            -int(item.get("member_count", 0)),
    98→            item.get("id", ""),
    99→        )
   100→
   101→    if item.get("kind") == "subjective_dimension" or item.get("is_subjective"):
   102→        return (
   103→            int(item.get("effective_tier", 4)),
   104→            1,  # Subjective items sort after mechanical items within T4.
   105→            subjective_score_value(item),
   106→            item.get("id", ""),
   107→        )
   108→
   109→    detail = detail_dict(item)
   110→    review_weight = float(item.get("review_weight", 0.0) or 0.0)
   111→    return (
   112→        int(item.get("effective_tier", item.get("tier", 3))),
   113→        0,
   114→        CONFIDENCE_ORDER.get(item.get("confidence", "low"), 9),
   115→        -review_weight,
   116→        -int(detail.get("count", 0) or 0),
   117→        item.get("id", ""),
   118→    )
   119→
   120→
   121→def item_explain(item: dict) -> dict:
   122→    effective_tier = int(item.get("effective_tier", item.get("tier", 3)))
   123→
   124→    if item.get("kind") == "subjective_dimension":
   125→        return {
   126→            "kind": "subjective_dimension",
   127→            "effective_tier": effective_tier,
   128→            "subjective_score": subjective_score_value(item),
   129→            "policy": (
   130→                "Subjective dimensions are always queued as T4 and do not outrank "
   131→                "mechanical T1/T2/T3 items."
   132→            ),
   133→            "ranking_factors": ["tier asc", "subjective_score asc", "id asc"],
   134→        }
   135→
   136→    detail = detail_dict(item)
   137→    confidence = item.get("confidence", "low")
   138→    is_subjective = bool(item.get("is_subjective"))
   139→    is_review = bool(item.get("is_review"))
   140→    ranking_factors: list[str]
   141→    if is_subjective:
   142→        ranking_factors = ["tier fixed to T4", "subjective_score asc", "id asc"]
   143→    elif is_review:
   144→        ranking_factors = [
   145→            "tier asc",
   146→            "confidence asc",
   147→            "review_weight desc",
   148→            "count desc",
   149→            "id asc",
   150→        ]
   151→    else:
   152→        ranking_factors = ["tier asc", "confidence asc", "count desc", "id asc"]
   153→    explain = {
   154→        "kind": "finding",
   155→        "effective_tier": effective_tier,
   156→        "confidence": confidence,
   157→        "confidence_rank": CONFIDENCE_ORDER.get(confidence, 9),
   158→        "count": int(detail.get("count", 0) or 0),
   159→        "id": item.get("id", ""),
   160→        "ranking_factors": ranking_factors,
   161→    }
   162→    if is_review:
   163→        explain["review_weight"] = float(item.get("review_weight", 0.0) or 0.0)
   164→    if is_subjective:
   165→        explain["policy"] = (
   166→            "Subjective findings are forced to T4 and do not outrank "
   167→            "mechanical T1/T2/T3 items."
   168→        )
   169→        explain["subjective_score"] = subjective_score_value(item)
   170→    return explain
   171→
   172→
   173→def tier_counts(items: list[dict]) -> dict[int, int]:
   174→    counts = {1: 0, 2: 0, 3: 0, 4: 0}
   175→    for item in items:
   176→        if item.get("kind") == "cluster":
   177→            # Count each member at its own tier
   178→            for member in item.get("members", []):
   179→                tier = int(member.get("effective_tier", member.get("tier", 3)))
   180→                counts[tier] = counts.get(tier, 0) + 1
   181→            continue
   182→        tier = int(item.get("effective_tier", item.get("tier", 3)))
   183→        counts[tier] = counts.get(tier, 0) + 1
   184→    return counts
   185→
   186→
   187→def choose_fallback_tier(requested_tier: int, counts: dict[int, int]) -> int | None:
   188→    available = [tier for tier, count in counts.items() if count > 0]
   189→    if not available:
   190→        return None
   191→    return min(available, key=lambda tier: (abs(tier - requested_tier), tier))
   192→
   193→
   194→def group_queue_items(items: list[dict], group: str) -> dict[str, list[dict]]:
   195→    """Group queue items for alternate output modes."""
   196→    grouped: dict[str, list[dict]] = {}
   197→    for item in items:
   198→        if group == "file":
   199→            key = item.get("file", "")
   200→        elif group == "detector":
   201→            key = item.get("detector", "")
   202→        elif group == "tier":
   203→            key = f"T{int(item.get('effective_tier', item.get('tier', 3)))}"
   204→        elif group == "cluster":
   205→            plan_cluster = item.get("plan_cluster")
   206→            key = plan_cluster["name"] if isinstance(plan_cluster, dict) else "(unclustered)"
   207→        else:
   208→            key = "items"
   209→        grouped.setdefault(key, []).append(item)
   210→    return grouped
   211→
   212→
   213→__all__ = [
   214→    "build_finding_items",
   215→    "choose_fallback_tier",
   216→    "item_explain",
   217→    "item_sort_key",
   218→    "tier_counts",
   219→    "subjective_score_value",
   220→    "group_queue_items",
   221→]
   222→

1→"""Helper utilities for work queue item construction."""
     2→
     3→from __future__ import annotations
     4→
     5→import re
     6→from fnmatch import fnmatch
     7→
     8→from desloppify.core.enums import finding_status_tokens
     9→from desloppify.core.registry import DETECTORS
    10→from desloppify.engine.planning.scorecard_projection import (
    11→    scorecard_subjective_entries,
    12→)
    13→from desloppify.intelligence.integrity import (
    14→    is_holistic_subjective_finding,
    15→    unassessed_subjective_dimensions,
    16→)
    17→from desloppify.scoring import DISPLAY_NAMES
    18→
    19→ALL_STATUSES = set(finding_status_tokens(include_all=True))
    20→ATTEST_EXAMPLE = (
    21→    "I have actually [DESCRIBE THE CONCRETE CHANGE YOU MADE] "
    22→    "and I am not gaming the score by resolving without fixing."
    23→)
    24→
    25→
    26→def detail_dict(item: dict) -> dict:
    27→    """Return finding detail as a dict; tolerate legacy/non-dict payloads."""
    28→    detail = item.get("detail")
    29→    return detail if isinstance(detail, dict) else {}
    30→
    31→
    32→def status_matches(item_status: str, status_filter: str) -> bool:
    33→    return status_filter == "all" or item_status == status_filter
    34→
    35→
    36→def is_subjective_finding(item: dict) -> bool:
    37→    detector = item.get("detector")
    38→    if detector in {"subjective_assessment"}:
    39→        return True
    40→    if detector == "holistic_review":
    41→        return True
    42→    return False
    43→
    44→
    45→def is_review_finding(item: dict) -> bool:
    46→    return item.get("detector") == "review"
    47→
    48→
    49→def review_finding_weight(item: dict) -> float:
    50→    """Return review issue weight aligned with issues list ordering."""
    51→    confidence = str(item.get("confidence", "low")).lower()
    52→    weight_by_confidence = {
    53→        "high": 1.0,
    54→        "medium": 0.7,
    55→        "low": 0.3,
    56→    }
    57→    weight = weight_by_confidence.get(confidence, 0.3)
    58→    if detail_dict(item).get("holistic"):
    59→        weight *= 10.0
    60→    return float(weight)
    61→
    62→
    63→def scope_matches(item: dict, scope: str | None) -> bool:
    64→    """Apply show-style pattern matching against a queue item."""
    65→    if not scope:
    66→        return True
    67→
    68→    item_id = item.get("id", "")
    69→    detector = item.get("detector", "")
    70→    filepath = item.get("file", "")
    71→    summary = item.get("summary", "")
    72→    dimension = detail_dict(item).get("dimension_name", "")
    73→    kind = item.get("kind", "")
    74→
    75→    if "*" in scope:
    76→        return any(
    77→            fnmatch(candidate, scope)
    78→            for candidate in (item_id, filepath, detector, dimension, summary)
    79→        )
    80→
    81→    if "::" in scope:
    82→        return item_id.startswith(scope)
    83→
    84→    lowered = scope.lower()
    85→    if kind == "subjective_dimension":
    86→        return (
    87→            lowered in item_id.lower()
    88→            or lowered in dimension.lower()
    89→            or lowered in summary.lower()
    90→        )
    91→
    92→    # Hash suffix: 8+ hex chars matches the tail segment of a finding ID.
    93→    if len(lowered) >= 8 and re.fullmatch(r"[0-9a-f]+", lowered):
    94→        return item_id.lower().endswith("::" + lowered)
    95→
    96→    return (
    97→        detector == scope
    98→        or filepath == scope
    99→        or filepath.startswith(scope.rstrip("/") + "/")
   100→    )
   101→
   102→
   103→def slugify(text: str) -> str:
   104→    return re.sub(r"[^a-z0-9_]+", "_", text.lower()).strip("_")
   105→
   106→
   107→def _canonical_subjective_dimension_key(display_name: str) -> str:
   108→    """Map a display label (e.g. 'Mid elegance') to its canonical dimension key."""
   109→    cleaned = display_name.replace(" (subjective)", "").strip()
   110→    target = cleaned.lower()
   111→
   112→    for dim_key, label in DISPLAY_NAMES.items():
   113→        if str(label).lower() == target:
   114→            return str(dim_key)
   115→    return slugify(cleaned)
   116→
   117→
   118→def _subjective_dimension_aliases(display_name: str) -> set[str]:
   119→    """Return normalized aliases used to match display labels with finding dimension keys."""
   120→    cleaned = display_name.replace(" (subjective)", "").strip()
   121→    canonical = _canonical_subjective_dimension_key(cleaned)
   122→    return {
   123→        cleaned.lower(),
   124→        cleaned.replace(" ", "_").lower(),
   125→        slugify(cleaned),
   126→        canonical.lower(),
   127→        slugify(canonical),
   128→    }
   129→
   130→
   131→def supported_fixers_for_item(state: dict, item: dict) -> set[str] | None:
   132→    """Return supported fixers for an item's language when known."""
   133→    lang = str(item.get("lang", "") or "").strip()
   134→    if not lang:
   135→        return None
   136→
   137→    caps = state.get("lang_capabilities", {})
   138→    if not isinstance(caps, dict):
   139→        return None
   140→
   141→    lang_caps = caps.get(lang, {})
   142→    if not isinstance(lang_caps, dict):
   143→        return None
   144→
   145→    fixers = lang_caps.get("fixers")
   146→    if not isinstance(fixers, list):
   147→        return None
   148→    return {fixer for fixer in fixers if isinstance(fixer, str)}
   149→
   150→
   151→def primary_command_for_finding(
   152→    item: dict, *, supported_fixers: set[str] | None = None
   153→) -> str:
   154→    detector = item.get("detector", "")
   155→    meta = DETECTORS.get(detector)
   156→    if meta and meta.action_type == "auto_fix" and meta.fixers:
   157→        available_fixers = [
   158→            fixer
   159→            for fixer in meta.fixers
   160→            if supported_fixers is None or fixer in supported_fixers
   161→        ]
   162→        if available_fixers:
   163→            return f"desloppify fix {available_fixers[0]} --dry-run"
   164→    if detector == "subjective_review":
   165→        if is_holistic_subjective_finding(item):
   166→            return "desloppify review --prepare"
   167→        return "desloppify show subjective"
   168→    return f'desloppify resolve fixed "{item.get("id", "")}" --note "<what you did>" --attest "{ATTEST_EXAMPLE}"'
   169→
   170→
   171→def subjective_strict_scores(state: dict) -> dict[str, float]:
   172→    dim_scores = state.get("dimension_scores", {}) or {}
   173→    if not dim_scores:
   174→        return {}
   175→
   176→    entries = scorecard_subjective_entries(state, dim_scores=dim_scores)
   177→    scores: dict[str, float] = {}
   178→    for entry in entries:
   179→        name = str(entry.get("name", "")).strip()
   180→        if not name:
   181→            continue
   182→        strict_val = float(entry.get("strict", entry.get("score", 100.0)))
   183→        [REDACTED](name)
   184→        aliases = _subjective_dimension_aliases(name)
   185→        for cli_key in entry.get("cli_keys", []):
   186→            key = str(cli_key).strip().lower()
   187→            if not key:
   188→                continue
   189→            aliases.add(key)
   190→            aliases.add(slugify(key))
   191→        aliases.add(dim_key.lower())
   192→        aliases.add(slugify(dim_key))
   193→        for alias in aliases:
   194→            scores[alias] = strict_val
   195→    return scores
   196→
   197→
   198→def build_subjective_items(
   199→    state: dict, findings: dict, *, threshold: float = 100.0
   200→) -> list[dict]:
   201→    """Create synthetic subjective work items (always tier 4)."""
   202→    dim_scores = state.get("dimension_scores", {}) or {}
   203→    if not dim_scores:
   204→        return []
   205→    threshold = max(0.0, min(100.0, float(threshold)))
   206→
   207→    subjective_entries = scorecard_subjective_entries(state, dim_scores=dim_scores)
   208→    if not subjective_entries:
   209→        return []
   210→    unassessed_dims = {
   211→        str(name).strip()
   212→        for name in unassessed_subjective_dimensions(
   213→            dim_scores
   214→        )
   215→    }
   216→
   217→    # Review findings are keyed by raw dimension name (snake_case).
   218→    review_open_by_dim: dict[str, int] = {}
   219→    for finding in findings.values():
   220→        if finding.get("status") != "open" or finding.get("detector") != "review":
   221→            continue
   222→        dim_key = str(detail_dict(finding).get("dimension", "")).strip().lower()
   223→        if not dim_key:
   224→            continue
   225→        review_open_by_dim[dim_key] = review_open_by_dim.get(dim_key, 0) + 1
   226→
   227→    items: list[dict] = []
   228→    for entry in subjective_entries:
   229→        name = str(entry.get("name", "")).strip()
   230→        if not name:
   231→            continue
   232→        strict_val = float(entry.get("strict", entry.get("score", 100.0)))
   233→        if strict_val >= threshold:
   234→            continue
   235→
   236→        [REDACTED](name)
   237→        aliases = set(_subjective_dimension_aliases(name))
   238→        cli_keys = [
   239→            str(key).strip().lower()
   240→            for key in entry.get("cli_keys", [])
   241→            if str(key).strip()
   242→        ]
   243→        aliases.update(cli_keys)
   244→        aliases.update(slugify(key) for key in cli_keys)
   245→        open_review = sum(review_open_by_dim.get(alias, 0) for alias in aliases)
   246→        is_unassessed = bool(entry.get("placeholder")) or (
   247→            name in unassessed_dims
   248→            or (strict_val <= 0.0 and int(entry.get("issues", 0)) == 0)
   249→        )
   250→        is_stale = bool(entry.get("stale"))
   251→        if is_unassessed:
   252→            primary_command = "desloppify review --prepare"
   253→        elif is_stale:
   254→            if cli_keys:
   255→                primary_command = (
   256→                    "desloppify review --prepare --dimensions " + ",".join(cli_keys)
   257→                )
   258→            else:
   259→                primary_command = "desloppify review --prepare"
   260→        else:
   261→            if open_review > 0:
   262→                primary_command = "desloppify show review --status open"
   263→            elif cli_keys:
   264→                primary_command = (
   265→                    "desloppify review --prepare --dimensions " + ",".join(cli_keys)
   266→                )
   267→            else:
   268→                primary_command = "desloppify review --prepare"
   269→        stale_tag = " [stale — re-review]" if is_stale else ""
   270→        summary = f"Subjective dimension below target: {name} ({strict_val:.1f}%){stale_tag}"
   271→        items.append(
   272→            {
   273→                "id": f"subjective::{slugify(dim_key)}",
   274→                "detector": "subjective_assessment",
   275→                "file": ".",
   276→                "tier": 4,
   277→                "effective_tier": 4,
   278→                "confidence": "medium",
   279→                "summary": summary,
   280→                "detail": {
   281→                    "dimension_name": name,
   282→                    "dimension": dim_key,
   283→                    "issues": int(entry.get("issues", 0)),
   284→                    "strict_score": strict_val,
   285→                    "open_review_findings": open_review,
   286→                    "cli_keys": cli_keys,
   287→                },
   288→                "status": "open",
   289→                "kind": "subjective_dimension",
   290→                "primary_command": primary_command,
   291→            }
   292→        )
   293→    return items
   294→
   295→
   296→__all__ = [
   297→    "ALL_STATUSES",
   298→    "ATTEST_EXAMPLE",
   299→    "build_subjective_items",
