Now I have all the data I need. Here is my critique.

---

## Discoverability Critique: desloppify --help

### Perspective: A brand-new AI agent (or human) encountering this tool for the first time, armed only with `--help` output.

---

### 1. Could I figure out the basic workflow from --help alone?

**Yes, and this is the strongest part of the help system.** The top-level `--help` does three things well:

- The one-liner "Desloppify -- codebase health tracker" immediately tells me what this tool is.
- The commands are **grouped by role** ("core workflow", "investigation", "maintenance", "setup & admin") rather than just listed alphabetically. This is unusual for CLI tools and extremely helpful. At a glance I can see the five core commands: `scan`, `status`, `next`, `resolve`, `plan`.
- The **examples section** at the bottom gives me a realistic taste of actual usage patterns.

The `plan --help` output is even better -- it has a "typical workflow" section that literally spells out the loop: scan, plan, queue, cluster, focus, next, done. That is exactly the information a new user needs.

**Verdict: The basic workflow is discoverable.** I could figure out "scan to find problems, plan/next to prioritize, resolve/done to mark complete" within about 60 seconds of reading.

---

### 2. How do I see what's wrong with my codebase?

**Clear.** The top-level help says `scan` will "Run all detectors, update state, show diff." The `scan --help` is minimal but sufficient -- `desloppify scan` with no arguments is a valid starting point, and auto-detection of language and path means I do not need to know anything else.

**Minor gap:** There is no mention in `scan --help` of what the output actually looks like. Will it print findings to stdout? Will it just update state silently? The phrase "show diff" implies it shows something, but a new user might wonder what to expect. A one-line note like "Prints a summary of new, resolved, and remaining findings to stdout" would help.

**Another gap:** The `--profile` options (`objective`, `full`, `ci`) are not explained. What is the difference between "objective" and "full"? Which is the default? A new user has to guess.

---

### 3. How do I prioritize work?

**Mostly clear, but with some confusion between overlapping commands.** There are multiple ways to see prioritized work:

- `plan` -- "full prioritized markdown"
- `plan queue` -- "compact table of all items"
- `next` -- "show next highest-priority item"
- `status` -- "score dashboard with per-tier progress"

The **tier system** (T1-T4) is well-explained in `next --help` with the clear labels: critical, important, moderate, cosmetic. This is very good -- a new user immediately knows what the tiers mean.

**Point of confusion:** The relationship between `plan` and `next` is not obvious. Both seem to answer "what should I work on?" The distinction appears to be that `plan` is a broader view (markdown document) while `next` is a single-item recommendation. But there is also `plan queue` which seems to overlap with `next --count 30`. When would I use one vs. the other? This is never explicitly stated.

**Point of confusion:** `plan done` vs `resolve fixed`. Both seem to mark things as done. The `plan --help` says `done` "requires attestation, purges from plan" while `resolve` is listed as a core workflow command. Which should I use? The `plan done` help suggests it is the preferred way in the plan workflow, but `resolve` is listed as a core command. A new user would be uncertain.

---

### 4. How do I mark things as done?

**This is the weakest area.** There are at least three ways to mark things as resolved:

1. `desloppify resolve fixed <pattern> --attest "..."` (core workflow command)
2. `desloppify plan done <id> --attest "..."` (plan subcommand)
3. `desloppify plan skip <id>` (for things you don't want to fix)

The **attestation requirement** is the biggest potential source of confusion and frustration. The `resolve --help` says `--attest` is "Required" and must include BOTH keywords "I have actually" and "not gaming." This is clearly documented in the help text, which is good. But a new user encountering this for the first time would likely:
- Fail on the first attempt (forgetting `--attest` or using the wrong wording)
- Feel the requirement is heavy-handed without understanding the rationale

The help text does not explain **why** attestation exists. A single line like "Attestation prevents score inflation from resolving findings without actually fixing them" would make this less annoying.

**The examples are helpful here.** The `resolve` examples show the exact syntax including the `--attest` flag, so a copy-paste user would succeed.

---

### What was the clearest part?

1. **The grouped command listing** in the top-level help. Categorizing commands into "core workflow", "investigation", "maintenance", and "setup & admin" is excellent UX. Most CLI tools just dump an alphabetical list.

2. **The tier system** in `next --help`. The four-line block (T1 critical, T2 important, T3 moderate, T4 cosmetic) is concise and immediately useful.

3. **The "typical workflow" block** in `plan --help`. This is the single most useful piece of help text in the entire tool. It walks through the exact sequence: scan, plan, queue, cluster, focus, next, done.

4. **The examples sections.** Every command that has examples benefits enormously from them. The examples in `resolve`, `plan move`, and the top-level help are realistic and show actual ID/pattern syntax.

---

### What was the least clear?

1. **Overlap between `plan done` and `resolve fixed`.** These appear to do similar things. There is no guidance on when to use which. Are they aliases? Does one call the other? Does using one break the other?

2. **What "findings" and "IDs" look like.** The help text references patterns like `"unused::src/foo.tsx::React"` in examples, which is helpful, but never explains the ID format. A new user seeing `PATTERN` in the usage line and "Finding ID(s), prefix, detector name, file path, or glob" in the description might wonder: what does a finding ID actually look like? The `::` delimiter format is only revealed through examples.

3. **The `--state` flag** appears on almost every command but is never explained beyond "Path to state file." A new user would wonder: Do I need this? Where is the default? What is a state file? (The answer is almost certainly "you never need it," but the help does not say that.)

4. **`status` help is extremely bare.** Just `--state` and `--json`. No description of what the dashboard shows, no examples. Compare this to the richness of `plan --help`.

5. **The `scan` profile options** (`objective`, `full`, `ci`) are listed but not described. Which is the default? What does each include or exclude?

6. **No mention of `.desloppify/` directory.** The tool presumably creates a state directory, but the help never mentions this. A new user running `scan` for the first time might be surprised by new files appearing.

---

### Missing information that would have helped

- **A "Getting Started" flow** in the top-level help or a `--quickstart` flag. Something like: "First time? Run: `desloppify scan`, then `desloppify next`."
- **What the state directory is and where it lives.** Even one line: "State is persisted in `.desloppify/` in your project root."
- **Default values** for key options. What is the default `--profile`? What is the default `--count` for `next`? (This one is actually documented -- "default: 1" -- good.)
- **The relationship between `plan done` and `resolve fixed`** should be explicitly stated somewhere.
- **A brief explanation of the finding ID format** (e.g., `detector::file::symbol`).

---

### Overall Discoverability Rating: 7.5 / 10

**Strengths:**
- Grouped command listing is best-in-class for CLI tools
- The "typical workflow" in `plan --help` is exactly what a new user needs
- Examples are realistic and use actual syntax
- The tier system is clearly explained
- Commands have descriptive one-liners in the top-level help

**Weaknesses:**
- Overlapping resolution commands (`resolve fixed` vs `plan done`) without guidance on which to use
- Several commands have very sparse help (`status`, `scan --profile`)
- The finding ID format is only discoverable through examples, not explicitly documented
- The attestation requirement's rationale is unexplained
- No mention of the `.desloppify/` state directory or what gets persisted

**Bottom line:** A competent user or agent could figure out the scan-plan-next-resolve workflow from `--help` alone within a few minutes. The grouped commands and workflow examples carry most of the weight. The main friction points are the overlapping resolution commands and a few under-documented options. This is well above average for a CLI tool, but there is room to tighten up the edges.

All 8 commands have been executed. Here is my detailed critique:

---

## Critique: Desloppify Queue Management Workflow

### 1. Was the queue output clear enough to understand what to work on?

**Mostly yes, with caveats.** The `plan queue` output is well-structured with a numbered table showing rank, tier, detector, summary, and cluster. The top-line summary `Queue: 266 items (T1:7 T2:107 T3:439 T4:260)` is immediately useful for understanding scope.

However, there are several clarity issues:

- **Tier ranges like "T1-T2" are confusing.** The first item shows `T1-T2 unused [3 items]`. Does this mean the cluster contains a mix of T1 and T2 findings? That is a reasonable inference, but it is not explained anywhere. When I filter `--tier 1`, this item still appears, and when I filter `--tier 2`, it appears again. This is correct behavior, but the tier-range notation is never explained.

- **Summaries are generic and unhelpful.** "Fix 16 file issues", "Fix 36 phantom read issues", "Fix 7 overload issues" -- these tell me the count and detector sub-category, but give me zero information about *what* is actually wrong. Compare with item 13 from the tier-2 filter: `Exact dupe cluster (4 functions, 100% similar)` -- that is actually descriptive. The auto-cluster summaries should include more context (e.g. which files are affected, or what the actual smell is).

- **The truncation with ellipsis is a problem.** Items like `Fix 36 high cyclomatic complexity is...` get cut off. The column seems too narrow for the content it holds.

- **No file paths visible in queue view.** I can see detector names and cluster names, but I cannot see *which files* are affected. For deciding what to work on, file paths are critical context. Even showing the top 1-2 files per cluster would help enormously.

### 2. Could you figure out how to reorder/prioritize from the help?

**Yes, the `plan move` help is actually excellent.** The examples are clear and practical:
```
desloppify plan move security top
desloppify plan move "unused::src/foo.ts::*" top
desloppify plan move smells bottom
desloppify plan move unused before security
```

This covers the common cases well. The positional argument structure `PATTERN {top,bottom,before,after,up,down} [target]` makes sense. The `up`/`down` with numeric offset is a nice touch.

**One gap:** The PATTERN argument accepts "Finding ID(s), prefix, detector name, file path, or glob" but the queue output does not show finding IDs at all. If I wanted to move a specific item (not an entire detector), I would have no idea what the finding ID looks like or how to obtain it. There is no `plan queue --verbose` or `plan queue --ids` flag to reveal them.

### 3. Was the relationship between `plan queue`, `plan show`, and `next` clear?

**No, this is the weakest point of the UX.**

- `plan queue` shows a ranked table of work items (clusters and singletons) with position numbers.
- `plan show` shows plan metadata: ordered items count, cluster list, overrides count, superseded count.
- `plan` (no subcommand) generates a "full prioritized markdown plan" (not tested but described in help).
- `next` shows the single highest-priority item.

The confusion:
- **`plan show` and `plan queue` overlap significantly.** Both show clusters. `plan show` lists them in what appears to be a different order than `plan queue`. The `plan show` output lists 36 clusters with their item counts and summaries. The `plan queue` output shows 30 items (with 236 more hidden). Are these the same items in different views? It is not immediately clear.
- **`plan show` says "Ordered items: 12" but `plan queue` shows 266 items.** What are the 12 "ordered" items? Are those manually reordered items? This is completely unexplained.
- **"Overrides: 4311" in `plan show` is opaque.** What is an override? The number is enormous (larger than the queue itself). No explanation is provided.
- **"Superseded: 22" is similarly unexplained.** Presumably these are findings that were resolved by other work, but this is just a guess.
- The `plan show` cluster list includes `auto/subjective_review: 230 items` and `auto/security: 3733 items`, but these do not appear in `plan queue` at all. Are they filtered out? Deferred? Overridden? The discrepancy is jarring and unexplained.

### 4. Any confusing overlaps or gaps between commands?

**Overlaps:**
- `plan show` vs `plan queue` vs `plan cluster list` -- three different ways to see clusters, each showing different information. It is unclear when to use which.
- `plan` (no subcommand) vs `plan queue` -- the help says bare `plan` generates a full markdown plan. How does this relate to `plan queue`?

**Gaps:**
- **No way to see individual findings within a cluster from `plan queue`.** I see "Fix 36 phantom read issues" but cannot drill down without knowing to run `plan cluster show auto/dict_keys-phantom_read`.
- **No way to see finding IDs.** The `plan move` command accepts finding IDs but nothing in the queue output shows them.
- **No `plan queue --expand` or similar** to show individual items within clusters.
- **No explanation of the scoring/ranking algorithm.** Why is "unused" ranked #1 and "facade" ranked #2? Tier is part of it, but within the same tier, the ordering rationale is invisible.
- **Tier filtering includes cross-tier clusters.** When I filter `--tier 1`, I get 4 items, all marked `T1-T2`. There are apparently no pure-T1 items. The header still shows `T1:7` which is confusing -- where are the 7 T1 items if I can only see 4 clusters?

### 5. Were cluster numbers/items/tiers displayed clearly enough?

**Partially.**

- Item counts within clusters are clear: `[3 items]`, `[16 items]`, etc.
- Cluster names are shown but use an internal `auto/` prefix notation that is not explained.
- The tier display is the weakest part. The `T1-T2` ranges obscure what is actually in the cluster. A breakdown like `T1:1, T2:2` would be much more useful.
- The total item counts in the header `(T1:7 T2:107 T3:439 T4:260)` sum to 813, but the queue says 266 items. This is because the queue collapses clusters, but the header counts individual findings. This mismatch is confusing and never explained.

### 6. What would make this workflow smoother?

1. **Show finding IDs in queue output** (or at least provide `--ids` flag). The move command needs them but the queue doesn't show them.
2. **Add a `plan queue --expand` mode** that shows individual findings within each cluster, with file paths and finding IDs.
3. **Explain the header counts.** The `Queue: 266 items (T1:7 ...)` line should clarify that 266 is the number of queue *entries* (clusters + singletons) while the tier breakdown counts individual findings. Or better: show both numbers.
4. **Improve summaries.** "Fix 16 file issues" is not actionable. Include the top 2-3 affected files, or the specific smell type.
5. **Clarify `plan show` output.** What are "Ordered items"? What are "Overrides"? Add one-line explanations or a legend.
6. **Explain why security/subjective_review clusters appear in `plan show` but not `plan queue`.** Are they deferred? Tier 4? Overridden? The user should not have to guess.
7. **Add cross-references.** `plan queue` output should suggest `plan cluster show <name>` for drilling down. `plan show` should suggest `plan queue` for the ranked view.
8. **Consistent ordering.** `plan show` and `plan queue` should present clusters in the same order, or explicitly state they use different orderings.
9. **Add `--file` filter to queue.** Let users filter the queue by file path, not just tier.
10. **Show ranking rationale.** Even a simple score column would help explain why items are ordered the way they are.

### 7. Rating: 5/10

**The bones are good but the polish is lacking.** The command structure is logical (`plan queue`, `plan move`, `plan cluster`, `plan focus`), the `move` command has excellent help text with examples, and the tier filtering works. But the actual *information density* of the outputs is too low for practical use. The generic summaries, missing finding IDs, unexplained metadata counts, and silent omissions of large clusters (security, subjective_review) make it hard to go from "looking at the queue" to "actually reordering things" without already knowing the internals of the system. A user encountering this for the first time would be able to *see* a list, but would struggle to make informed prioritization decisions based on what they see.

The disconnect between the header counts (813 individual findings across tiers) and the queue count (266 entries) is a particularly sharp UX paper cut -- it makes you distrust the numbers, which is the opposite of what a prioritization tool should do.

## Critique: "Work on the Next Thing" Workflow

### 1. Was it clear what the "next" item is and why it was chosen?

**Mostly yes, but the "why" is implicit.** The `next` command surfaces a clear item: "Remove 3 unused findings" with specific files and sample finding IDs. The tier navigator at the top (T1:7 T2:107 T3:439 T4:260) gives context about what's out there. The item is labeled "(Auto-fixable batch, 3 findings)" which signals both why it was chosen (auto-fixable items get priority) and the scope.

However, there is no explicit sentence explaining *why* this is item #1. The ranking logic is opaque -- you have to infer that auto-fixable + low finding count + high tier = top priority. When I filtered with `--tier 1`, I got the same item, which was slightly confusing because the queue view showed this item as "T1-T2" (a range), suggesting it spans tiers. A brief "Why this is next: auto-fixable, 3 findings across T1-T2, highest impact-per-effort" line would close this gap.

### 2. Is the relationship between `next` and `plan queue` obvious?

**Reasonably clear.** The two views are complementary:
- `plan queue --top 5` is a compact table: rank, tier, detector, summary, cluster name. Good for scanning the landscape.
- `next --count 5` is a detailed card view: files, sample IDs, action commands, resolve commands.

The items appear in the same order, which is good -- the queue is the table of contents, `next` is the full page. The numbering (`[1/5]`, `[2/5]`) in `next --count 5` helps orient you.

**One issue:** the queue shows items 1-5 with a note "... and 261 more (use --top 0 to show all)" which is helpful. But `next --count 5` doesn't hint that it's showing the same queue in a different format. A user might think these are two different prioritization systems. A brief header like "Showing top 5 from the plan queue (266 items total)" on the `next` output would tie them together.

### 3. Was it clear how to resolve/skip/done an item after seeing it?

**Yes, this is a strength.** Each `next` item card includes three concrete commands:
- `Resolve all:` with the full `desloppify resolve fixed` command including the cluster pattern
- `Drill in:` to see more items in the same cluster
- `Skip cluster:` to skip the whole group

These are copy-pasteable, which is excellent for an agent workflow. The resolve command even includes the attestation template with the placeholder text to fill in.

### 4. Were there too many ways to "complete" something?

**Yes, this is the most confusing aspect of the workflow.** There are at least three ways to mark something done:

1. `desloppify resolve fixed "auto/unused" --note "<what>" --attest "..."` (the command shown by `next`)
2. `desloppify plan done <PATTERN> --attest "..."` (a separate command)
3. `desloppify plan skip <PATTERN>` with various flags (`--permanent`, `--false-positive`, or temporary)

The relationship between `resolve fixed` and `plan done` is unclear. Looking at the help text:
- `resolve fixed` takes `--note` and `--attest`, operates on the state file, changes finding status
- `plan done` takes `--attest`, operates on the plan

Do I need to call both? Does `resolve fixed` automatically mark the plan item as done? Does `plan done` automatically change finding status? This is the critical question, and there is no documentation visible in the output that answers it. If `resolve fixed` is the "real" command and `plan done` is just a plan-management alias, it should say so. If they are independent, the workflow should explain when to use each.

Similarly, `plan skip` (temporary) vs `resolve wontfix` vs `plan skip --permanent` overlap conceptually. The `skip` help shows both `--permanent` (which requires `--note` and `--attest`) and a bare skip (just `--reason`). This maps to different severity levels of "I'm not doing this", which is thoughtful, but the user has to understand the scoring implications to choose correctly.

### 5. Any confusion about the attestation requirement?

**The template is helpful but verbose.** The attestation string shown by `next` is:

```
"I have actually [DESCRIBE THE CONCRETE CHANGE YOU MADE] and I am not gaming the score by resolving without fixing."
```

The `resolve --help` explains the two required keywords: "I have actually" and "not gaming". This is a reasonable anti-gaming mechanism. However:

- For an AI agent, the boilerplate is friction that adds no real safety (the agent will just fill in the template mechanically).
- For a human, the mandatory phrasing feels patronizing -- you're forced to write "I am not gaming the score" every time.
- The `plan done --help` says `--attest` is "required" but `plan skip` says it's only required for `--permanent` and `--false-positive`. This inconsistency is fine but requires reading the help carefully.

The resolve command shown by `next` includes `--note "<what>"` AND `--attest "..."` -- that's two free-text arguments. Could these be collapsed into one? The note is the useful part; the attestation is the ceremony part.

### 6. What's the smoothest path from "see item -> work on it -> mark done"?

The smoothest path today is:

1. `desloppify next` -- see what's next
2. Do the work (e.g., remove the unused imports)
3. Copy-paste the `Resolve all:` command from the `next` output, filling in the note and attestation

This is a 3-step workflow, which is good. The friction points are:
- Filling in the attestation boilerplate (mechanical, not thoughtful)
- Wondering whether you also need to run `plan done` or if `resolve fixed` handles plan updates
- The `--note` value needing to be manually written even for auto-fixable items (could it be pre-filled for auto-fix cases?)

**Ideal flow would be:**
1. `desloppify next` -- see what's next (shows the item + one command to run when done)
2. Fix the issue
3. `desloppify done auto/unused --note "removed 3 unused imports"` -- single command, handles both state + plan

### 7. Additional observations

**The `--group detector` view is useful but disorienting.** It showed 20 items grouped by detector, but the tier labels all said "T3 [high]" for every single item except the first. If everything is the same priority, grouping doesn't help distinguish what to do first. It would be more useful if grouping preserved the queue rank number.

**The tier filtering (`--tier 1`) returned the same item as the default.** This suggests either the top item spans T1-T2, or the filtering isn't working as expected. The queue showed it as "T1-T2" which is unusual -- most users think of tiers as discrete. A note like "This cluster contains findings across T1 and T2" would clarify.

**The north star line is excellent.** "strict 90.4/100 -> target 98.0 (+7.6 needed)" gives a clear progress indicator. The subjective reminder at the bottom is also good -- it surfaces a parallel dimension of work without overwhelming.

**Warning messages at the top are noisy.** Both "Tool code changed" and "skill document outdated" appeared on every command. For an agent doing 8 commands in a row, this is distracting. These should probably be shown once per session or suppressed with a flag.

### Rating: 6.5/10

**What works well:**
- The `next` card format with copy-pasteable commands is excellent
- Queue and next are complementary and show the same ordering
- Tier navigator gives at-a-glance scope
- North star metric is motivating
- Skip/drill-in options are well-placed

**What holds it back:**
- The `resolve fixed` vs `plan done` distinction is the biggest source of confusion -- it's unclear if these are redundant, complementary, or operate on different state
- Attestation ceremony is friction without proportional benefit, especially for AI agents
- No explicit "why is this #1" explanation
- Warning noise on every invocation
- The grouped view flattens all priorities to the same level, reducing its utility
- The path from seeing an item to marking it done requires understanding the resolve/plan/state model, which is not explained inline

The foundation is strong -- the information density is high, the commands are discoverable, and the card format is well-designed. The main gap is the conceptual model: a user needs to understand the relationship between "findings" (state), "plan items" (queue), and "clusters" (grouping) to use the workflow confidently, and that mental model is never explicitly presented.

## Detailed Critique: Desloppify Investigation Workflow

### 1. Could you quickly understand the health of the codebase?

**Yes, largely.** The `status` command gives a strong at-a-glance picture. The score breakdown (overall/objective/strict/verified) with the accompanying guide is helpful. The dimension table with progress bars, tiers, and action labels is immediately readable. The "biggest weighted drags" section at the bottom is excellent -- it tells you exactly where the pain is without requiring mental arithmetic.

However, there is significant information overload. The `status` output is extremely long -- it includes scores, the dimension table, the score recipe breakdown, the structural debt by area, the agent plan footer, review summary, stale dimension commands, and workflow instructions. For a quick "how are we doing?" check, you have to wade through a lot. The output is probably 80+ lines for what should be a 15-second glance. A `--brief` flag or a compact default that expands with `--verbose` would help.

The "Subjective Measures" section with [stale] tags is somewhat confusing on first encounter. The footnote explaining stale means "assessment outdated, not an unresolved finding" helps, but it would be clearer if stale dimensions were visually de-emphasized rather than displayed alongside fresh data.

### 2. Was it clear how to drill down from overview to specific findings?

**Partially.** The path from `status` (overview) to `show <detector>` (findings by detector) to `show <file>` (findings by file) works well once you know it. The `status` output helpfully suggests `desloppify next` and `desloppify plan` as next steps.

What is *not* clear is how to get from a dimension in the status table (e.g., "File health: 90.0%") to the actual findings driving that dimension down. The dimension names in `status` (e.g., "File health", "Code quality") don't directly correspond to detector names you'd use with `show`. You would need to guess that "Code quality" maps to the `smells` detector, or that "File health" maps to `structural` + `facade`. There is no `show --dimension file_health` to bridge this gap. The `plan` markdown output does break things down by tier and lists specific findings, but you have to read a long document to find the relevant ones.

### 3. Is `plan queue` vs `status` vs `plan` (full markdown) clear in their different purposes?

**Not entirely.** There is meaningful overlap and the delineation is fuzzy:

- **`status`**: Dashboard overview with scores, dimension health, structural debt by area, and coaching. It is the richest surface.
- **`plan`**: Full markdown report with the same dimension health table (duplicated from `status`), plus the ordered queue and the full tier breakdown. Feels like a superset of `status` in a different format.
- **`plan queue`**: Compact clustered view of work items, sorted by priority. This is the most action-oriented view.

The problem is that `status` and `plan` share significant content (the dimension health table appears in both), while serving slightly different audiences. `status` is the quick check; `plan` is the full written report. But `status` is already so long that it doesn't feel "quick," and `plan` duplicates most of its header content.

`plan queue` is the clearest in its purpose -- it is a pure work queue. The `--tier` filter is well designed and immediately useful. This was the most satisfying command to run in the whole sequence.

### 4. Was the `show` command flexible enough for investigation?

**Mostly yes, with some gaps.** The `show` command accepts file paths, detector names, globs, and finding IDs as the positional `pattern` argument. The `--status`, `--top`, `--chronic`, and `--code` flags cover the most common filtering needs. Being able to do `show smells --top 10` and then pivot to `show desloppify/engine/_work_queue/core.py` is natural.

**Gaps I noticed:**

- **No severity/confidence filter.** You can filter by detector, file, and status, but not by confidence (high/medium/low) or tier. If I want to see "all T2 findings across the codebase," I have to use `plan queue --tier 2` and then individually `show` each cluster. A `show --tier 2` would fill this gap.
- **No dimension filter.** As noted above, `show --dimension code_quality` doesn't exist but would be very useful for drilling down from the status dashboard.
- **The noise budget is confusing.** The output says "Noise budget: 10/detector (158 hidden: smells: +158)." This implies there are 168 total smells findings but only 10 are shown due to a noise budget. The `--top 10` flag I passed should be the limit, not an internal noise budget. It is unclear whether `--top 50` would override the noise budget or if I need a different flag. The interplay between `--top` and the noise budget is unexplained.
- **No `--sort` option.** Findings are sorted by file path. Being able to sort by severity, tier, or reopen count would be useful for prioritization.

### 5. Any missing views or filters you wished existed?

- **`show --tier N`** -- Filter findings by tier directly, bridging the gap between `plan queue` and `show`.
- **`show --dimension <name>`** -- Show all findings contributing to a specific dimension's health score.
- **`status --brief`** -- A compact 10-line summary: overall score, top 3 drags, open count, next action.
- **`plan queue --detector smells`** -- Filter the queue by detector. Currently `plan queue` only supports `--tier`.
- **`show --area desloppify/app`** -- The structural debt area breakdown in `status` lists areas like "desloppify/app (161 items)" but there is no obvious way to expand that into individual findings without manually passing the directory to `show`.
- **Cross-referencing chronic findings with the plan queue.** The `--chronic` view shows reopened findings, but there is no way to see if those chronic items are already in the plan queue or if they are orphaned.
- **A diff/trend view.** Something like `status --trend` showing score movement over the last N scans would help answer "are we getting better or worse?"

### 6. How well do the different commands complement each other vs overlap?

**Moderate complementation with notable overlap.** The intended workflow seems to be:

1. `status` -- Am I healthy? What dimensions need attention?
2. `plan` / `plan queue` -- What should I work on, and in what order?
3. `show` -- Drill into specific findings for a detector or file.
4. `next` -- Give me the single next thing to do.

This is a reasonable flow. The commands do complement each other at the conceptual level. The main overlap problems:

- **`status` and `plan`** share the dimension health table almost identically. The tier summary lines in `plan` are also derivable from `status`.
- **The "AGENT PLAN" footer** appears on every single command output (`show`, `status`, `plan queue`). It is the same block every time, occupying 5-6 lines of every output. After you have seen it once, it becomes noise. A `--quiet` flag or only showing it on `status`/`next` would reduce clutter.
- **The warning lines** about tool code changes and outdated skill documents also appear on every `show` invocation. These are scan-time warnings that should probably only appear on `scan` or `status`, not on read-only investigation commands.

### 7. Rating: Investigation Workflow Experience

**7/10**

**Strengths:**
- The dimension-based health model is genuinely useful. Seeing health as a percentage per dimension with action types is the right abstraction level.
- `plan queue` with `--tier` filtering is excellent. Clear, concise, actionable.
- `show` is versatile -- accepting file paths, detectors, globs, and finding IDs as the same positional argument is ergonomic.
- The `--chronic` flag is a thoughtful touch for surfacing persistent problems.
- Finding IDs being displayed inline makes it possible to reference specific findings in other commands.
- The "biggest weighted drags" section in `status` is the single best piece of output in the entire workflow.

**Weaknesses:**
- Information overload: `status` is too long, the AGENT PLAN footer repeats everywhere, and warning banners appear on investigation commands.
- The dimension-to-detector mapping gap makes drilling down from the overview non-obvious.
- The noise budget mechanism in `show` is opaque and potentially conflicts with `--top`.
- No tier or severity filtering in `show`.
- `status` vs `plan` overlap is confusing -- it is not immediately clear when you would use one vs the other.
- No trend/history view to answer "are things improving?"

The tool clearly has deep functionality and a well-thought-out data model. The investigation workflow gets you from "what's wrong" to "what specific finding in what file" in 2-3 commands, which is solid. The main area for improvement is output density management and filling the filtering gaps between the overview commands and the detail commands.

    45→        print_error(f"could not save state: {exc}")
    46→        sys.exit(1)
    47→
    48→
    49→def _save_config_or_exit(config: dict) -> None:
    50→    """Persist config with a consistent CLI error boundary."""
    51→    try:
    52→        config_mod.save_config(config)
    53→    except OSError as exc:
    54→        print_error(f"could not save config: {exc}")
    55→        sys.exit(1)
    56→
    57→
    58→def cmd_resolve(args: argparse.Namespace) -> None:
    59→    """Resolve finding(s) matching one or more patterns."""
    60→    attestation = getattr(args, "attest", None)
    61→    _validate_resolve_inputs(args, attestation)
    62→
    63→    state_file = state_path(args)
    64→    state = state_mod.load_state(state_file)
    65→    _enforce_batch_wontfix_confirmation(
    66→        state,
    67→        args,
    68→        attestation=attestation,
    69→        resolve_all_patterns_fn=_resolve_all_patterns,
    70→    )
    71→    prev = _previous_score_snapshot(state)
    72→    prev_subjective_scores = {
    73→        str(dim): (coerce_assessment_score(payload) or 0.0)
    74→        for dim, payload in (state.get("subjective_assessments") or {}).items()
    75→        if isinstance(dim, str)
    76→    }
    77→
    78→    all_resolved = _resolve_all_patterns(state, args, attestation=attestation)
    79→    if not all_resolved:
    80→        status_label = "resolved" if args.status == "open" else "open"
    81→        print(
    82→            colorize(
    83→                f"No {status_label} findings matching: {' '.join(args.patterns)}",
    84→                "yellow",
    85→            )
    86→        )
    87→        return
    88→
    89→    _save_state_or_exit(state, state_file)
    90→
    91→    # Remove resolved items from the living plan queue (best-effort)
    92→    try:
    93→        from desloppify.engine.plan import has_living_plan, load_plan, purge_ids, save_plan
    94→
    95→        if has_living_plan():
    96→            plan = load_plan()
    97→            purged = purge_ids(plan, all_resolved)
    98→            if purged:
    99→                save_plan(plan)
   100→                print(colorize(f"  Plan updated: {purged} item(s) removed from queue.", "dim"))
   101→    except (OSError, ValueError, KeyError, TypeError):
   102→        print(colorize("  Warning: could not update living plan.", "yellow"), file=sys.stderr)
   103→
   104→    _print_resolve_summary(status=args.status, all_resolved=all_resolved)
   105→    _print_wontfix_batch_warning(
   106→        state,
   107→        status=args.status,
   108→        resolved_count=len(all_resolved),
   109→    )
   110→    has_review_findings = any(
   111→        state["findings"].get(fid, {}).get("detector") == "review"
   112→        for fid in all_resolved
   113→    )
   114→    _print_score_movement(
   115→        status=args.status,
   116→        prev_overall=prev.overall,
   117→        prev_objective=prev.objective,
   118→        prev_strict=prev.strict,
   119→        prev_verified=prev.verified,
   120→        state=state,
   121→        has_review_findings=has_review_findings,
   122→    )
   123→    _print_subjective_reset_hint(
   124→        args=args,
   125→        state=state,
   126→        all_resolved=all_resolved,
   127→        prev_subjective_scores=prev_subjective_scores,
   128→    )
   129→
   130→    lang = resolve_lang(args)
   131→    lang_name = lang.name if lang else None
   132→    narrative = narrative_mod.compute_narrative(
   133→        state,
   134→        context=narrative_mod.NarrativeContext(lang=lang_name, command="resolve"),
   135→    )
   136→    if narrative.get("milestone"):
   137→        print(colorize(f"  → {narrative['milestone']}", "green"))
   138→
   139→    next_command = _print_next_command(state)
   140→    _write_resolve_query_entry(
   141→        ResolveQueryContext(
   142→            patterns=args.patterns,
   143→            status=args.status,
   144→            resolved=all_resolved,
   145→            next_command=next_command,
   146→            prev_overall=prev.overall,
   147→            prev_objective=prev.objective,
   148→            prev_strict=prev.strict,
   149→            prev_verified=prev.verified,
   150→            attestation=attestation,
   151→            narrative=narrative,
   152→            state=state,
   153→        )
   154→    )
   155→
   156→
   157→def cmd_ignore_pattern(args: argparse.Namespace) -> None:
   158→    """Add a pattern to the ignore list."""
   159→    attestation = getattr(args, "attest", None)
   160→    if not validate_attestation(attestation):
   161→        show_attestation_requirement("Ignore", attestation, ATTEST_EXAMPLE)
   162→        sys.exit(1)
   163→
   164→    runtime = command_runtime(args)
   165→    state_file = runtime.state_path
   166→    state = runtime.state
   167→    prev = state_mod.score_snapshot(state)
   168→
   169→    config = runtime.config
   170→    config_mod.add_ignore_pattern(config, args.pattern)
   171→    config["needs_rescan"] = True
   172→    _save_config_or_exit(config)
   173→
   174→    removed = state_mod.remove_ignored_findings(state, args.pattern)
   175→    state.setdefault("attestation_log", []).append(
   176→        {
   177→            "timestamp": state.get("last_scan"),
   178→            "command": "ignore",
   179→            "pattern": args.pattern,
   180→            "attestation": attestation,
   181→            "affected": removed,
   182→        }
   183→    )
   184→    _save_state_or_exit(state, state_file)
   185→
   186→    print(colorize(f"Added ignore pattern: {args.pattern}", "green"))
   187→    if removed:
   188→        print(f"  Removed {removed} matching findings from state.")
   189→    config_warning = check_config_staleness(config)
   190→    if config_warning:
   191→        print(colorize(f"  {config_warning}", "yellow"))
   192→    print_score_update(state, prev)
   193→
   194→    lang = resolve_lang(args)
   195→    lang_name = lang.name if lang else None
   196→    narrative = narrative_mod.compute_narrative(
   197→        state,
   198→        context=narrative_mod.NarrativeContext(lang=lang_name, command="ignore"),
   199→    )
   200→    scores = state_mod.score_snapshot(state)
   201→    write_query(
   202→        {
   203→            "command": "ignore",
   204→            "pattern": args.pattern,
   205→            "removed": removed,
   206→            "overall_score": scores.overall,
   207→            "objective_score": scores.objective,

   209→            "verified_strict_score": scores.verified,
   210→            "attestation": attestation,

   213→    )

   118→    if not finding_ids:

   154→    print(colorize(f"  {label[kind]} {count} item(s).", "green"))

   189→        print(colorize(f"  Reopened {len(reopened)} finding(s) in state.", "dim"))

   241→def cmd_plan_reopen(args: argparse.Namespace) -> None:
   242→    """Reopen resolved findings from plan context."""

   245→
   246→    patterns: list[str] = getattr(args, "patterns", [])

1→"""Pure mutation operations on the plan model."""
     2→
     3→from __future__ import annotations
     4→
     5→from desloppify.engine._plan.schema import (
     6→    Cluster,
     7→    PlanModel,
     8→    SkipEntry,
     9→    ensure_plan_defaults,
    10→)
    11→from desloppify.engine._state.schema import utc_now
    12→
    13→
    14→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    15→# Helpers
    16→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    17→
    18→def _remove_id_from_lists(plan: PlanModel, finding_id: str) -> None:
    19→    """Remove a finding ID from queue_order and skipped."""
    20→    order: list[str] = plan["queue_order"]
    21→    skipped: dict[str, SkipEntry] = plan.get("skipped", {})
    22→    if finding_id in order:
    23→        order.remove(finding_id)
    24→    skipped.pop(finding_id, None)
    25→
    26→
    27→def _resolve_position(
    28→    order: list[str],
    29→    position: str,
    30→    target: str | None = None,
    31→    offset: int | None = None,
    32→    finding_ids: list[str] | None = None,
    33→) -> int:
    34→    """Resolve a position specifier to an insertion index.
    35→
    36→    *finding_ids* are the IDs being moved — used to calculate relative
    37→    positions when they already exist in the list.
    38→    """
    39→    moving = set(finding_ids or [])
    40→
    41→    if position == "top":
    42→        return 0
    43→    if position == "bottom":
    44→        return len(order)
    45→
    46→    if position == "before" and target:
    47→        for i, item_id in enumerate(order):
    48→            if item_id == target and item_id not in moving:
    49→                return i
    50→        return 0
    51→
    52→    if position == "after" and target:
    53→        for i, item_id in enumerate(order):
    54→            if item_id == target and item_id not in moving:
    55→                return i + 1
    56→        return len(order)
    57→
    58→    if position == "up" and offset is not None:
    59→        if not finding_ids:
    60→            return 0
    61→        # Find current position of first item
    62→        first_id = finding_ids[0]
    63→        current_idx = None
    64→        clean_order = [x for x in order if x not in moving]
    65→        for i, item_id in enumerate(clean_order):
    66→            if item_id == first_id:
    67→                current_idx = i
    68→                break
    69→        if current_idx is None:
    70→            # Item not yet in order; treat as "insert at position offset from top"
    71→            return max(0, len(clean_order) - offset)
    72→        return max(0, current_idx - offset)
    73→
    74→    if position == "down" and offset is not None:
    75→        if not finding_ids:
    76→            return len(order)
    77→        first_id = finding_ids[0]
    78→        clean_order = [x for x in order if x not in moving]
    79→        current_idx = None
    80→        for i, item_id in enumerate(clean_order):
    81→            if item_id == first_id:
    82→                current_idx = i
    83→                break
    84→        if current_idx is None:
    85→            return len(clean_order)
    86→        return min(len(clean_order), current_idx + offset)
    87→
    88→    return len(order)
    89→
    90→
    91→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    92→# Move items
    93→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
    94→
    95→def move_items(
    96→    plan: PlanModel,
    97→    finding_ids: list[str],
    98→    position: str,
    99→    target: str | None = None,
   100→    offset: int | None = None,
   101→) -> int:
   102→    """Move finding IDs to a position in queue_order. Returns count moved."""
   103→    ensure_plan_defaults(plan)
   104→    order: list[str] = plan["queue_order"]
   105→
   106→    # Remove from skipped if present
   107→    skipped: dict[str, SkipEntry] = plan.get("skipped", {})
   108→    for fid in finding_ids:
   109→        skipped.pop(fid, None)
   110→
   111→    # Remove from current position in order
   112→    for fid in finding_ids:
   113→        if fid in order:
   114→            order.remove(fid)
   115→
   116→    # Resolve insertion point
   117→    idx = _resolve_position(order, position, target, offset, finding_ids)
   118→
   119→    # Insert in original order
   120→    for i, fid in enumerate(finding_ids):
   121→        order.insert(idx + i, fid)
   122→
   123→    return len(finding_ids)
   124→
   125→
   126→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   127→# Skip / unskip
   128→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   129→
   130→def skip_items(
   131→    plan: PlanModel,
   132→    finding_ids: list[str],
   133→    *,
   134→    kind: str = "temporary",
   135→    reason: str | None = None,
   136→    note: str | None = None,
   137→    attestation: str | None = None,
   138→    review_after: int | None = None,
   139→    scan_count: int = 0,
   140→) -> int:
   141→    """Move finding IDs to the skipped dict. Returns count skipped."""
   142→    ensure_plan_defaults(plan)
   143→    now = utc_now()
   144→    count = 0
   145→    skipped: dict[str, SkipEntry] = plan["skipped"]
   146→    for fid in finding_ids:
   147→        _remove_id_from_lists(plan, fid)
   148→        skipped[fid] = {
   149→            "finding_id": fid,
   150→            "kind": kind,
   151→            "reason": reason,
   152→            "note": note,
   153→            "attestation": attestation,
   154→            "created_at": now,
   155→            "review_after": review_after,
   156→            "skipped_at_scan": scan_count,
   157→        }
   158→        count += 1
   159→    return count
   160→
   161→
   162→def unskip_items(
   163→    plan: PlanModel, finding_ids: list[str]
   164→) -> tuple[int, list[str]]:
   165→    """Bring finding IDs back from skipped to the end of queue_order.
   166→
   167→    Returns ``(count_unskipped, permanent_ids_needing_state_reopen)``
   168→    where the second list contains IDs that were permanent or false_positive
   169→    and need their state-layer status reopened by the caller.
   170→    """
   171→    ensure_plan_defaults(plan)
   172→    count = 0
   173→    need_reopen: list[str] = []
   174→    skipped: dict[str, SkipEntry] = plan["skipped"]
   175→    for fid in finding_ids:
   176→        entry = skipped.pop(fid, None)
   177→        if entry is not None:
   178→            if entry.get("kind") in ("permanent", "false_positive"):
   179→                need_reopen.append(fid)
   180→            if fid not in plan["queue_order"]:
   181→                plan["queue_order"].append(fid)
   182→            count += 1
   183→    return count, need_reopen
   184→
   185→
   186→def resurface_stale_skips(
   187→    plan: PlanModel, current_scan_count: int
   188→) -> list[str]:
   189→    """Move temporary skips past their review_after threshold back to queue.
   190→
   191→    Returns list of resurfaced finding IDs.
   192→    """
   193→    ensure_plan_defaults(plan)
   194→    skipped: dict[str, SkipEntry] = plan["skipped"]
   195→    resurfaced: list[str] = []
   196→    for fid in list(skipped):
   197→        entry = skipped[fid]
   198→        if entry.get("kind") != "temporary":
   199→            continue
   200→        review_after = entry.get("review_after")
   201→        if review_after is None:
   202→            continue
   203→        skipped_at = entry.get("skipped_at_scan", 0)
   204→        if current_scan_count >= skipped_at + review_after:
   205→            skipped.pop(fid)
   206→            if fid not in plan["queue_order"]:
   207→                plan["queue_order"].append(fid)
   208→            resurfaced.append(fid)
   209→    return resurfaced
   210→
   211→
   212→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   213→# Describe / annotate
   214→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   215→
   216→def describe_finding(
   217→    plan: PlanModel, finding_id: str, description: str | None
   218→) -> None:
   219→    """Set or clear an augmented description on a finding."""
   220→    ensure_plan_defaults(plan)
   221→    now = utc_now()
   222→    overrides = plan["overrides"]
   223→    if finding_id not in overrides:
   224→        overrides[finding_id] = {"finding_id": finding_id, "created_at": now}
   225→    overrides[finding_id]["description"] = description
   226→    overrides[finding_id]["updated_at"] = now
   227→
   228→
   229→def annotate_finding(
   230→    plan: PlanModel, finding_id: str, note: str | None
   231→) -> None:
   232→    """Set or clear a note on a finding."""
   233→    ensure_plan_defaults(plan)
   234→    now = utc_now()
   235→    overrides = plan["overrides"]
   236→    if finding_id not in overrides:
   237→        overrides[finding_id] = {"finding_id": finding_id, "created_at": now}
   238→    overrides[finding_id]["note"] = note
   239→    overrides[finding_id]["updated_at"] = now
   240→
   241→
   242→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   243→# Clusters
   244→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   245→
   246→def create_cluster(
   247→    plan: PlanModel,
   248→    name: str,
   249→    description: str | None = None,
   250→    action: str | None = None,
   251→) -> Cluster:
   252→    """Create a named cluster. Raises ValueError if it already exists."""
   253→    ensure_plan_defaults(plan)
   254→    if name.startswith("auto/"):
   255→        raise ValueError(
   256→            f"Cluster names starting with 'auto/' are reserved for auto-clusters: {name!r}"
   257→        )
   258→    if name in plan["clusters"]:
   259→        raise ValueError(f"Cluster {name!r} already exists")
   260→    now = utc_now()
   261→    cluster: Cluster = {
   262→        "name": name,
   263→        "description": description,
   264→        "finding_ids": [],
   265→        "created_at": now,
   266→        "updated_at": now,
   267→        "auto": False,
   268→        "cluster_key": "",
   269→        "action": action,
   270→        "user_modified": False,
   271→    }
   272→    plan["clusters"][name] = cluster
   273→    return cluster
   274→
   275→
   276→def add_to_cluster(
   277→    plan: PlanModel, cluster_name: str, finding_ids: list[str]
   278→) -> int:
   279→    """Add finding IDs to a cluster. Returns count added."""
   280→    ensure_plan_defaults(plan)
   281→    cluster = plan["clusters"].get(cluster_name)
   282→    if cluster is None:
   283→        raise ValueError(f"Cluster {cluster_name!r} does not exist")
   284→
   285→    member_ids: list[str] = cluster["finding_ids"]
   286→    count = 0
   287→    now = utc_now()
   288→    for fid in finding_ids:
   289→        if fid not in member_ids:
   290→            member_ids.append(fid)
   291→            count += 1
   292→        # Update override to track cluster membership
   293→        overrides = plan["overrides"]
   294→        if fid not in overrides:
   295→            overrides[fid] = {"finding_id": fid, "created_at": now}
   296→        overrides[fid]["cluster"] = cluster_name
   297→        overrides[fid]["updated_at"] = now
   298→
   299→    cluster["updated_at"] = now
   300→    return count
   301→
   302→
   303→def remove_from_cluster(
   304→    plan: PlanModel, cluster_name: str, finding_ids: list[str]
   305→) -> int:
   306→    """Remove finding IDs from a cluster. Returns count removed."""
   307→    ensure_plan_defaults(plan)
   308→    cluster = plan["clusters"].get(cluster_name)
   309→    if cluster is None:
   310→        raise ValueError(f"Cluster {cluster_name!r} does not exist")
   311→
   312→    member_ids: list[str] = cluster["finding_ids"]
   313→    now = utc_now()
   314→    count = 0
   315→    for fid in finding_ids:
   316→        if fid in member_ids:
   317→            member_ids.remove(fid)
   318→            count += 1
   319→        # Clear cluster from override
   320→        override = plan["overrides"].get(fid)
   321→        if override and override.get("cluster") == cluster_name:
   322→            override["cluster"] = None
   323→            override["updated_at"] = now
   324→
   325→    # Mark auto-clusters as user_modified when items are manually removed
   326→    if count > 0 and cluster.get("auto"):
   327→        cluster["user_modified"] = True
   328→
   329→    cluster["updated_at"] = now
   330→    return count
   331→
   332→
   333→def delete_cluster(plan: PlanModel, name: str) -> list[str]:
   334→    """Delete a cluster and clear cluster refs from overrides. Returns orphaned IDs."""
   335→    ensure_plan_defaults(plan)
   336→    cluster = plan["clusters"].pop(name, None)
   337→    if cluster is None:
   338→        raise ValueError(f"Cluster {name!r} does not exist")
   339→
   340→    orphaned = list(cluster.get("finding_ids", []))
   341→    now = utc_now()
   342→    for fid in orphaned:
   343→        override = plan["overrides"].get(fid)
   344→        if override and override.get("cluster") == name:
   345→            override["cluster"] = None
   346→            override["updated_at"] = now
   347→
   348→    if plan.get("active_cluster") == name:
   349→        plan["active_cluster"] = None
   350→
   351→    return orphaned
   352→
   353→
   354→def move_cluster(
   355→    plan: PlanModel,
   356→    cluster_name: str,
   357→    position: str,
   358→    target: str | None = None,
   359→    offset: int | None = None,
   360→) -> int:
   361→    """Move all cluster members as a contiguous block. Returns count moved."""
   362→    ensure_plan_defaults(plan)
   363→    cluster = plan["clusters"].get(cluster_name)
   364→    if cluster is None:
   365→        raise ValueError(f"Cluster {cluster_name!r} does not exist")
   366→
   367→    member_ids = list(cluster.get("finding_ids", []))
   368→    if not member_ids:
   369→        return 0
   370→
   371→    return move_items(plan, member_ids, position, target, offset)
   372→
   373→
   374→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   375→# Focus
   376→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   377→
   378→def set_focus(plan: PlanModel, cluster_name: str) -> None:
   379→    """Set the active cluster focus."""
   380→    ensure_plan_defaults(plan)
   381→    if cluster_name not in plan["clusters"]:
   382→        raise ValueError(f"Cluster {cluster_name!r} does not exist")
   383→    plan["active_cluster"] = cluster_name
   384→
   385→
   386→def clear_focus(plan: PlanModel) -> None:
   387→    """Clear the active cluster focus."""
   388→    ensure_plan_defaults(plan)
   389→    plan["active_cluster"] = None
   390→
   391→
   392→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   393→# Reset
   394→# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
   395→
   396→def reset_plan(plan: PlanModel) -> None:
   397→    """Reset plan to empty state, preserving version and created timestamp."""
   398→    created = plan.get("created", utc_now())
   399→    plan.clear()
   400→    from desloppify.engine._plan.schema import empty_plan as _empty
   401→    for k, v in _empty().items():
   402→        plan[k] = v
   403→    plan["created"] = created
   404→
   405→
   406→def purge_ids(plan: PlanModel, finding_ids: list[str]) -> int:
   407→    """Remove finding IDs from the plan entirely.
   408→
   409→    Cleans queue_order, skipped, and all cluster memberships.
   410→    Does NOT touch overrides (descriptions/notes are kept for history).
   411→    Returns count of IDs that were actually present somewhere.
   412→    """
   413→    ensure_plan_defaults(plan)
   414→    found = 0
   415→
   416→    order: list[str] = plan["queue_order"]
   417→    skipped: dict[str, SkipEntry] = plan["skipped"]
   418→    for fid in finding_ids:
   419→        was_present = False
   420→        if fid in order:
   421→            order.remove(fid)
   422→            was_present = True
   423→        if fid in skipped:
   424→            skipped.pop(fid)
   425→            was_present = True
   426→        for cluster in plan.get("clusters", {}).values():
   427→            ids = cluster.get("finding_ids", [])
   428→            if fid in ids:
   429→                ids.remove(fid)
   430→                was_present = True
   431→        if was_present:
   432→            found += 1
   433→
   434→    return found
   435→
   436→
   437→__all__ = [
   438→    "add_to_cluster",
   439→    "annotate_finding",
   440→    "clear_focus",
   441→    "create_cluster",
   442→    "delete_cluster",
   443→    "describe_finding",
   444→    "move_cluster",
   445→    "move_items",
   446→    "purge_ids",
   447→    "remove_from_cluster",
   448→    "reset_plan",
   449→    "resurface_stale_skips",
   450→    "set_focus",
   451→    "skip_items",
   452→    "unskip_items",
   453→]
   454→
```

1→"""State-application helpers for resolve command flows."""
     2→
     3→from __future__ import annotations
     4→
     5→import argparse
     6→
     7→from desloppify import state as state_mod
     8→from desloppify.app.commands.helpers.query import write_query_best_effort as _write_query_best_effort
     9→from desloppify.core.output_contract import OutputResult
    10→
    11→from .selection import ResolveQueryContext
    12→
    13→
    14→def _try_expand_cluster(pattern: str) -> list[str] | None:
    15→    """If pattern matches a cluster name, return its finding IDs."""
    16→    try:
    17→        from desloppify.engine.plan import has_living_plan, load_plan
    18→
    19→        if not has_living_plan():
    20→            return None
    21→        plan = load_plan()
    22→        cluster = plan.get("clusters", {}).get(pattern)
    23→        if cluster and cluster.get("finding_ids"):
    24→            return list(cluster["finding_ids"])
    25→    except (OSError, ValueError, KeyError, TypeError):
    26→        pass
    27→    return None
    28→
    29→
    30→def _resolve_all_patterns(
    31→    state: dict,
    32→    args: argparse.Namespace,
    33→    *,
    34→    attestation: str | None,
    35→) -> list[str]:
    36→    all_resolved: list[str] = []
    37→    for pattern in args.patterns:
    38→        # Check if pattern is a cluster name — expand to member IDs
    39→        cluster_ids = _try_expand_cluster(pattern)
    40→        if cluster_ids:
    41→            for fid in cluster_ids:
    42→                resolved = state_mod.resolve_findings(
    43→                    state,
    44→                    fid,
    45→                    args.status,
    46→                    args.note,
    47→                    attestation=attestation,
    48→                )
    49→                all_resolved.extend(resolved)
    50→            continue
    51→
    52→        resolved = state_mod.resolve_findings(
    53→            state,
    54→            pattern,
    55→            args.status,
    56→            args.note,
    57→            attestation=attestation,
    58→        )
    59→        all_resolved.extend(resolved)
    60→    return all_resolved
    61→
    62→
    63→def write_query(payload: dict) -> OutputResult:
    64→    """Backward-compatible resolve query writer seam."""
    65→    return _write_query_best_effort(
    66→        payload,
    67→        context="resolve query payload update",
    68→    )
    69→
    70→
    71→def _write_resolve_query_entry(context: ResolveQueryContext) -> None:
    72→    scores = state_mod.score_snapshot(context.state)
    73→    write_query(
    74→        {
    75→            "command": "resolve",
    76→            "patterns": context.patterns,
    77→            "status": context.status,
    78→            "resolved": context.resolved,
    79→            "count": len(context.resolved),
    80→            "next_command": context.next_command,
    81→            "overall_score": scores.overall,
    82→            "objective_score": scores.objective,
    83→            "strict_score": scores.strict,
    84→            "verified_strict_score": scores.verified,
    85→            "prev_overall_score": context.prev_overall,
    86→            "prev_objective_score": context.prev_objective,
    87→            "prev_strict_score": context.prev_strict,
    88→            "prev_verified_strict_score": context.prev_verified,
    89→            "attestation": context.attestation,
    90→            "narrative": context.narrative,
    91→        }
    92→    )
    93→

What's the right fix — should we show both numbers? Change what tier_counts reports? Show item counts differently?

    11→]
    12→
    13→from desloppify.core._internal.text_utils import is_numeric
    14→from desloppify.engine._state.filtering import _matches_pattern
    15→from desloppify.engine._state.schema import (
    16→    StateModel,
    17→    ensure_state_defaults,
    18→    utc_now,
    19→    validate_state_invariants,
    20→)
    21→from desloppify.engine._state.scoring import _recompute_stats
    22→
    23→
    24→def coerce_assessment_score(value: object) -> float | None:
    25→    """Normalize a subjective assessment score payload to a 0-100 float.
    26→
    27→    Returns ``None`` when the value cannot be interpreted as a numeric score
    28→    (e.g. bools, non-numeric strings, missing keys).
    29→    """
    30→    if is_numeric(value):
    31→        return round(max(0.0, min(100.0, float(value))), 1)
    32→    if isinstance(value, dict):
    33→        raw = value.get("score")
    34→        if not is_numeric(raw):
    35→            return None
    36→        return round(max(0.0, min(100.0, float(raw))), 1)
    37→    return None
    38→
    39→
    40→def _mark_stale_assessments_on_review_resolve(
    41→    state: StateModel,
    42→    *,
    43→    status: str,
    44→    resolved_findings: list[dict],
    45→    now: str,
    46→) -> None:
    47→    """Mark subjective assessments as stale when review findings are resolved.
    48→
    49→    The assessment score is preserved (not zeroed) — only a fresh review import
    50→    should change dimension scores.  The stale marker tells the UI to prompt
    51→    for a re-review.
    52→    """
    53→    assessments = state.get("subjective_assessments")
    54→    if not isinstance(assessments, dict) or not assessments:
    55→        return
    56→
    57→    touched_dimensions: set[str] = set()
    58→    for finding in resolved_findings:
    59→        if finding.get("detector") != "review":
    60→            continue
    61→        dimension = str(finding.get("detail", {}).get("dimension", "")).strip()
    62→        if dimension:
    63→            touched_dimensions.add(dimension)
    64→
    65→    for dimension in sorted(touched_dimensions):
    66→        if dimension not in assessments:
    67→            continue
    68→
    69→        payload = assessments.get(dimension)
    70→        if isinstance(payload, dict):
    71→            payload["needs_review_refresh"] = True
    72→            payload["refresh_reason"] = f"review_finding_{status}"
    73→            payload["stale_since"] = now
    74→        else:
    75→            assessments[dimension] = {
    76→                "score": coerce_assessment_score(payload) or 0.0,
    77→                "needs_review_refresh": True,
    78→                "refresh_reason": f"review_finding_{status}",
    79→                "stale_since": now,
    80→            }
    81→
    82→
    83→def match_findings(
    84→    state: StateModel, pattern: str, status_filter: str = "open"
    85→) -> list[dict]:
    86→    """Return findings matching *pattern* with the given status."""
    87→    ensure_state_defaults(state)
    88→    return [
    89→        finding
    90→        for finding_id, finding in state["findings"].items()
    91→        if not finding.get("suppressed")
    92→        if (status_filter == "all" or finding["status"] == status_filter)
    93→        and _matches_pattern(finding_id, finding, pattern)
    94→    ]
    95→
    96→
    97→def resolve_findings(
    98→    state: StateModel,
    99→    pattern: str,
   100→    status: str,
   101→    note: str | None = None,
   102→    attestation: str | None = None,
   103→) -> list[str]:
   104→    """Set finding status for matches and return affected finding IDs."""
   105→    ensure_state_defaults(state)
   106→    now = utc_now()
   107→    resolved: list[str] = []
   108→    resolved_findings: list[dict] = []
   109→    status_filter = "all" if status == "open" else "open"
   110→    for finding in match_findings(state, pattern, status_filter=status_filter):
   111→        previous_status = str(finding.get("status", "open")).strip() or "open"
   112→        if status == "open" and previous_status == "open":
   113→            continue
   114→
   115→        extra_updates: dict[str, object] = {}
   116→        if status == "wontfix":
   117→            snapshot_scan_count = int(state.get("scan_count", 0) or 0)
   118→            extra_updates["wontfix_scan_count"] = snapshot_scan_count
   119→            extra_updates["wontfix_snapshot"] = {
   120→                "captured_at": now,
   121→                "scan_count": snapshot_scan_count,
   122→                "tier": finding.get("tier"),
   123→                "confidence": finding.get("confidence"),
   124→                "detail": copy.deepcopy(finding.get("detail", {})),
   125→            }
   126→        if status == "open":
   127→            finding["reopen_count"] = int(finding.get("reopen_count", 0) or 0) + 1
   128→            finding.pop("wontfix_scan_count", None)
   129→            finding.pop("wontfix_snapshot", None)
   130→            previous_note = finding.get("note")
   131→            next_note = note if note is not None else previous_note
   132→            extra_updates["resolved_at"] = None
   133→            extra_updates["note"] = next_note
   134→            extra_updates["resolution_attestation"] = {
   135→                "kind": "manual_reopen",
   136→                "text": attestation or note,
   137→                "attested_at": now,
   138→                "scan_verified": False,
   139→                "previous_status": previous_status,
   140→            }
   141→
   142→        updates: dict[str, object] = {
   143→            "status": status,
   144→            "note": note,
   145→            "resolved_at": now,
   146→            "suppressed": False,
   147→            "suppressed_at": None,
   148→            "suppression_pattern": None,
   149→            "resolution_attestation": {
   150→                "kind": "manual",
   151→                "text": attestation,
   152→                "attested_at": now,
   153→                "scan_verified": False,
   154→            },
   155→        }
   156→        updates.update(extra_updates)
   157→        finding.update(updates)
   158→        resolved.append(finding["id"])
   159→        resolved_findings.append(finding)
   160→
   161→    _mark_stale_assessments_on_review_resolve(
   162→        state,
   163→        status=status,
   164→        resolved_findings=resolved_findings,
   165→        now=now,
   166→    )
   167→
   168→    _recompute_stats(state, scan_path=state.get("scan_path"))
   169→    validate_state_invariants(state)
   170→    return resolved
   171→
```

    13→)

    24→
    25→@dataclass(frozen=True)
    26→class QueueBuildOptions:
    27→    """Configuration for queue construction and tier selection behavior."""
    28→
    29→    tier: int | None = None
    30→    count: int | None = 1
    31→    scan_path: str | None = None
    32→    scope: str | None = None
    33→    status: str = "open"
    34→    include_subjective: bool = True
    35→    subjective_threshold: float = 100.0
    36→    chronic: bool = False
    37→    no_tier_fallback: bool = False
    38→    explain: bool = False
    39→    plan: dict | None = None
    40→    include_skipped: bool = False
